Lectures on Twisted Rabinowitz–Floer Homology
Yannis Bähni

Abstract
Rabinowitz–Floer homology is the Morse–Bott homology in the sense of Floer associated with the Rabinowitz action functional introduced by Kai Cieliebak and Urs Frauenfelder in 2009. In this manuscript, we consider a generalisation of this theory to a Rabinowitz–Floer homology of a Liouville automorphism. As an application, we show the existence of noncontractible periodic Reeb orbits on quotients of symmetric star-shaped hypersurfaces. In particular, this theory applies to lens spaces. Moreover, we prove a forcing theorem, which guarantees the existence of a contractible twisted closed characteristic on a displaceable twisted stable hypersurface in a symplectically aspherical geometrically bounded symplectic manifold if there exists a contractible twisted closed characteristic belonging to a Morse-Bott component, with energy difference smaller or equal to the displacement energy of the displaceable hypersurface.



1
Introduction

The existence of closed Reeb orbits on lens spaces is important in the study of celestial mechanics. Indeed, by
[
FK18
, Corollary 5.7.5]
, the Moser regularised energy hypersurface near the earth or the moon of the planar circular restricted three-body problem for energy values below the first critical value is diffeomorphic to the real projective space
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
. See also
[
HS16
, Introduction]
for more details. Our main result will be the following.

Theorem 1.1 (
[
Bä21
, Theorem 1.2]
).

Let
\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
n\geq 2
, be a compact and connected star-shaped hypersurface invariant under the rotation

\varphi\colon\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}^{n},\quad\varphi(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\bigl(e^{2\pi ik_{1}/m}z_{1},\dots,e^{2\pi ik_{n}/m}z_{n}\bigr)}

for some even
m\geq 2
and
k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
coprime to
m
. Then
\Sigma/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
admits a noncontractible periodic Reeb orbit.

Theorem
1.1
has similarities with the following two recent results.

Theorem 1.2 (
[
Gra+20
, Corollary 1.6 (iv)]
).

Any contact form on
\mathbb{S}^{2n-1}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
defining the standard contact structure admits a closed Reeb orbit.

Using the fact that there is a natural bijection between contact forms on the odd-dimensional sphere equipped with the standard contact structure and star-shaped hypersurfaces, Theorem
1.2
is actually stronger than Theorem
1.1
in that it does not restrict the parity of the lens space. However, Theorem
1.2
does not say anything about the topological nature of the Reeb orbit. The proof of this theorem uses a generalisation of Givental’s nonlinear Maslov index to lens spaces.

Theorem 1.3 (
[
HL21
, Theorem 1.2]
).

Every dynamically convex star-shaped
C^{3}
-hypersurface
\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
n\geq 2
, satisfying
\Sigma=-\Sigma
admits at least two symmetric geometrically distinct closed characteristics.

Theorem
1.3
has the advantage of being a
multiplicity result
, but in disadvantage requires the assumption that the hypersurface is dynamically convex and does only work for
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-symmetry. To the authors knowledge, the first named author is working on extending Theorem
1.3
to lens spaces. As many multiplicity results, the proof of this theorem makes use of index theory and in particular Ekeland–Hofer theory. The proof of Theorem
1.1
relies on a generalisation of
Rabinowitz–Floer homology
. Rabinowitz–Floer homology is the Morse–Bott homology in the sense of Floer associated with the Rabinowitz action functional introduced by Kai Cieliebak and Urs Frauenfelder in 2009. See the excellent survey article
[
AF12a
]
for a brief introduction to Rabinowitz–Floer homology and
[
AS19
]
for an overview of common Floer theories. One important feature of this homology in our work is that it provides an affirmative answer to the
Weinstein conjecture
in some instances. Specifically, we introduce an analogue of the twisted Floer homology
[
Ulj17
]
in the Rabinowitz–Floer setting. Following
[
CF09
]
and
[
AF10
]
, we construct a Morse–Bott homology for a suitable twisted version of the standard Rabinowitz action functional, that is, the Lagrange multiplier functional of the symplectic area functional.

Theorem 1.4 (
[
Bä21
, Theorem 1.1]
).

Let
(M,\lambda)
be the completion of a Liouville domain
(W,\lambda)
and let
\varphi\in\Diff(W)
be of finite order and such that
\varphi^{*}\lambda-\lambda=df_{\varphi}
for some smooth compactly supported function
f_{\varphi}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Int W)
.

(a)

The semi-infinite dimensional Morse–Bott homology
\RFH^{\varphi}(\partial W,M)
in the sense of Floer of the twisted Rabinowitz action functional exists and is well-defined. Moreover, twisted Rabinowitz–Floer homology is invariant under twisted homotopies of Liouville domains.

(b)

If
\partial W
is simply connected and does not admit any nonconstant twisted Reeb orbit, then
\RFH^{\varphi}_{*}(\partial W,M)\cong\operatorname{H}_{*}(\Fix(\varphi|_{\partial W});\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.

(c)

If
\partial W
is displaceable by a compactly supported Hamiltonian symplectomorphism in
(M,\lambda)
, then
\RFH^{\varphi}(\partial W,M)\cong 0
.

Twisted Rabinowitz–Floer homology does indeed generalise standard Rabinowitz–Floer homology as

\RFH^{\id_{W}}(\partial W,M)\cong\RFH(\partial W,M).

The proof of Theorem
1.1
is straightforward, once we have computed the
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-equivariant twisted Rabinowitz–Floer homology of the spheres
\mathbb{S}^{2n-1}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Indeed, by invariance we may assume that
\Sigma=\mathbb{S}^{2n-1}
, as
\Sigma
is star-shaped. Then we use the following elementary topological fact (see Lemma
1.1
below). Let
\Sigma
be a simply connected topological manifold and let
\varphi\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
be a homeomorphism of finite order
m
that is not equal to the identity. If the induced discrete action

\mathbb{Z}_{m}\times\Sigma\to\Sigma,\qquad[k]\cdot x\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\varphi^{k}(x)

is free, then
\pi\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
is a normal covering map
[
Lee11
, Theorem 12.26]
. For a point
x\in\Sigma
define the
based twisted loop space of
\Sigma
and
\varphi
by

\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}(\Sigma,x)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\{\gamma\in C(I,\Sigma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\gamma(0)=x\text{ and }\gamma(1)=\varphi(x)\}},

where
I\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{[0,1]}
. Then we have the following result. See Figure
1
.

Lemma 1.1.

If
\gamma\in\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}(\Sigma,x)
, then
\pi\circ\gamma\in\mathscr{L}(\Sigma/\mathbb{Z}_{m},\pi(x))
is not contractible. Conversely, if
\gamma\in\mathscr{L}(\Sigma/\mathbb{Z}_{m},\pi(x))
is not contractible, then there exists
1\leq k<m
such that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{x}\in\mathscr{L}_{\varphi^{k}}(\Sigma,x)
for the unique lift
\widetilde{\gamma}_{x}
of
\gamma
with
\widetilde{\gamma}_{x}(0)=x
.

For a more detailed study of twisted loop spaces of universal covering manifolds as well as a proof of Lemma
1.1
see Appendix
A
.

Another interesting application of Theorem
1.4
is the following
forcing result
. Suppose that
\partial W
is Hamiltonianly displaceable in the completion
(M,\lambda)
and simply connected. If
\Fix(\varphi|_{\partial W})\neq\varnothing
, then
\partial W
does admit a twisted periodic Reeb orbit. Indeed,
if there does not exist any twisted periodic Reeb orbit on
\partial W
, we compute

\RFH^{\varphi}(\partial W,M)\cong\operatorname{H}(\Fix(\varphi|_{\partial W});\mathbb{Z}_{2})=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}\operatorname{H}_{k}(\Fix(\varphi|_{\partial W});\mathbb{Z}_{2})\neq 0

by part (b) of Theorem
1.4
, contradicting part (c) of Theorem
1.4
.

Figure 1
:
The projection
\pi\circ\gamma\in\mathscr{L}(\Sigma/\mathbb{Z}_{m},\pi(x))
of
\gamma\in\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}(\Sigma,x)
is not contractible for the deck transformation
\varphi\neq\id_{\Sigma}
.

Theorem 1.5 (Forcing).

Let
\Sigma
be a twisted stable displaceable hypersurface in a symplectically aspherical, geometrically bounded, symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
for some
\varphi\in\Symp(M,\omega)
and suppose that
v_{0}
is a contractible twisted closed characteristic on
\Sigma
belonging to a Morse–Bott component
C
. Then there exists a contractible twisted closed characteristic
v\notin C
such that

\int_{\mathbb{D}}\overline{v}^{*}\omega-\int_{\mathbb{D}}\overline{v}_{0}^{*}\omega\leq\ord(\varphi)e(\Sigma),

where
e(\Sigma)
denotes the displacement energy of
\Sigma
.

The proof of Theorem
1.5
is an adaptation of
[
CFP10
, Theorem 4.9]
. This result was initially shown by Felix Schlenk using quite different methods.

Finally, we put Theorem
1.1
into context. If
\Sigma^{2n-1}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
satisfies the index condition

\mu_{\CZ}(\gamma)>4-n

(1)

for all contractible Reeb orbits
\gamma
, the
\bigvee
-shaped symplectic homology
\check{\SH}(\Sigma)
can be defined in the positive cylindrical end
\mathinner{\left[0,+\infty\right)}\times\Sigma
by
[
Ueb19
, Corollary 3.7]
. If
\Sigma/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
admits a Liouville filling
W
, then we have

\check{\SH}_{*}(\Sigma/\mathbb{Z}_{m},M)\cong\RFH_{*}(\Sigma/\mathbb{Z}_{m},M),

where
M
denotes the completion of
W
. Note that even in the case of lens spaces this need not be the case, as for example
\mathbb{RP}^{2n-1}
is not Liouville fillable for any odd
n\geq 2
by
[
GN20
, Theorem 1.1]
. As the index condition (
1
) is only required for contractible Reeb orbits and they come from the universal covering manifold
\Sigma
, we can say something in the case where
\Sigma
is strictly convex. Indeed, the Hofer–Wysocki–Zehnder Theorem
[
FK18
, Theorem 12.2.1]
then implies that
\Sigma
is dynamically convex, that is,

\mu_{\CZ}(\gamma)\geq n+1

holds for all periodic Reeb orbits
\gamma
. Thus for
n\geq 2
, the index condition is satisfied and we can compute
\check{\SH}_{*}(\mathbb{S}^{2n-1}/\mathbb{Z}_{m})
via the
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-equivariant version of the symplectic homology
\check{\SH}_{*}(\mathbb{S}^{2n-1})
.

In the case of hypertight contact manifolds, there is a similar construction without the index condition (
1
). See for example
[
MN18
, Theorem 1.1]
. By
[
MN18
, Theorem 1.7]
, there do exist noncontractible periodic Reeb orbits on hypertight contact manifolds under suitable technical conditions. Moreover, one can show the existence of invariant Reeb orbits in this setting. See
[
MN18
, Corollary 1.6]
as well as
[
MN16
, Theorem 1.6]
in the Liouville-fillable case.

The thesis is organised as follows. In Section
2
, we review the basics of Hamiltonian Floer homology defined as the Morse–Bott homology associated with the symplectic action functional. We follow the cascade approach introduced by Urs Frauenfelder and define Hamiltonian Floer homology in the simplest case, that is, in the symplectically aspherical case. This is sufficient for our purposes. A detailed proof of the compactness of the relevant moduli spaces is given in Appendix
D
and in order to deal with transversality, we use the polyfold approach which is sketched in Appendix
C
. We also review stable Hamiltonian manifolds, a generalisation of contact manifolds. Appendix
D
is based on lecture notes written for a course on Hamiltonian Floer homology in the winter semester 2021/2022 taught by Urs Frauenfelder at the university of Augsburg.

In Section
3
, we introduce the main machinery for defining our new homology theory and prove Theorem
1.4
. This material is an extended version of
[
Bä21
]
.

In Section
4
, we prove Theorem
1.1
and the Forcing theorem
1.5
. Theorem
1.1
and its proof is also taken from
[
Bä21
]
.

In the final Chapter
5
, we indicate two further results that may be obtained using the theory developed in this thesis.

2
Hamiltonian Floer Homology

Floer homology was introduced by Andreas Floer around 1988 to tackle the
homological Arnold conjecture
. Roughly speaking, the conjecture says in its simplest form that the number of nondegenerate solutions of a
1
-periodic Hamiltonian equation is bounded below by the dimension of the singular homology of the symplectic manifold with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. That is, the number of such solutions is always bounded below by a topological invariant. This resembles the famous
Morse inequalities
, and thus it is not suprising that the construction of Floer homology was largely influenced by Morse homology. See the excellent article
[
AS19
]
. However, a key technical ingredient for this semi-infinite dimensional version of Morse homology was Gromov’s analysis of pseudoholomorphic curves introduced in 1985. Today there are many flavours of Floer theories, and we shall focus on
Rabinowitz–Floer homology
. This homology was introduced in 2009 by Kai Cieliebak and Urs Frauenfelder. Crucial is the observation, that Floer homology can also be constructed in a more general way, namely in the
Morse–Bott
case. In contrast to standard Hamiltonian Floer homology, Rabinowitz–Floer homology considers a fixed energy but arbitrary period problem. This leads to particular instances of the
Weinstein conjecture
formulated in 1979, including the result of Rabinowitz in 1978. Weinstein conjectured that on every compact manifold admitting a contact form, there must exist a closed Reeb orbit. For an extensive historical treatment see
[
MS17
]
.

The aim of this introductory chapter is to explain the fundamental concepts required later on. In the first section we discuss the finite-dimensional version of Morse–Bott homology, a generalisation of Morse homology.

The second section discusses some basic facts coming from Hamiltonian dynamics, focusing on theory we need in subsequent chapters.

The third section introduces the archetypical version of Floer homology,
Hamiltonian Floer homology
, on compact symplectic manifolds. We discuss the Morse–Bott approach, as this one will be useful in the discussion of Rabinowitz–Floer homology.

In the last section we discuss suitable structures on regular hypersurfaces in symplectic manifolds, including hypersurfaces of restricted contact type.

2.1
Morse–Bott Homology

Morse–Bott homology is a generalisation of Morse homology to functions with degenerate critical points. See
[
CF09
, Appendix A]
for a short introduction via the cascade approach and
[
Fra04
, Appendix A]
for a more extensive treatment. Morse–Bott functions often occur in the presence of symmetries. Indeed, let
G
be a Lie group acting on a manifold
M
. If
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
is
G
-invariant, that is,
f(gx)=f(x)
holds for all
g\in G
and
x\in M
, then
\Crit f
is also
G
-invariant. In particular,
f
is not a Morse function in general. However, the presence of symmetry usually simplifies the explicit computation of the Morse homology and symmetries occur throughout Physics.

Definition 2.1 (Morse–Bott Function,
[
MS17
, p. 232]
).

Let
M
be a smooth manifold. A
Morse–Bott function on
M
is defined to be a function
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that

(i)

\Crit f\subseteq M
is an embedded submanifold.

(ii)

T_{x}\Crit f=\ker\Hess f|_{x}
for all
x\in\Crit f
.

Remark 2.1.

Assumption (ii) is crucial for proving the Morse–Bott Lemma
[
BH04
, Lemma 3.51]
, an analogue of the Morse Lemma. The Morse–Bott Lemma is a key technical ingredient for proving exponential decay of gradient flow lines.

Example 2.1 (
\mathbb{S}^{2n-1}
).

Let
f\colon\mathbb{S}^{2n-1}\to\mathbb{R}
on the odd-dimensional sphere

\mathbb{S}^{2n-1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\Biggl\{(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathinner{\lvert z_{j}\rvert}^{2}=1\Biggr\}}

be defined by

f(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}j\mathinner{\lvert z_{j}\rvert}^{2}.

Then
f
is a Morse–Bott function with critical manifold being a disjoint union of
n
copies of
\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

Let
(M,g)
be a compact Riemannian manifold and
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
a Morse–Bott function. Choose an additional Morse function
h\in C^{\infty}(\Crit f)
and a Riemannian metric
g_{0}
on
\Crit f
such that
(h,g_{0})
is a Morse–Smale pair, that is, the stable and unstable manifolds intersect transversally. Using Theorem
C.1
, one can define a nonnegative
\mathbb{Z}
-graded chain complex
(\CM_{\ast}(f),\partial_{\ast})
of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-vector spaces by

\CM_{k}(f)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Crit_{k}h\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2},\hskip 11.49994pt\Crit_{k}h\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\{x\in\Crit h\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\ind_{f}(x)+\ind_{h}(x)=k\}},

for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
\ind
denotes the ordinary Morse index, that is, the number of negative eigenvalues of the Hessian at that point, with boundary operator

\partial_{k}\colon\CM_{k}(f)\to\CM_{k-1}(f),\hskip 22.99988pt\partial_{k}x^{-}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{x^{+}\in\Crit_{k-1}h}n(x^{-},x^{+})x^{+},

where

n(x^{-},x^{+})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\#_{2}\mathscr{M}(x^{-},x^{+})\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}

denotes the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-count of the abstractly perturbed unparametrised negative gradient flow lines with cascades from
x^{-}
to
x^{+}
. Then
\partial_{\ast}
is indeed a boundary operator by

\displaystyle(\partial_{k}\circ\partial_{k+1})x^{-}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x^{+}\in\Crit_{k-1}h}\sum_{x\in\Crit_{k}h}\#_{2}\mathscr{M}(x^{-},x)\#_{2}\mathscr{M}(x,x^{+})x^{+}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x^{+}\in\Crit_{k-1}h}\#_{2}\partial\mathscr{M}(x^{-},x^{+})x^{+}

\displaystyle=0

for all
x^{-}\in\Crit_{k+1}h
as

\partial\mathscr{M}(x^{-},x^{+})\cong\coprod_{x\in\Crit h}\mathscr{M}(x^{-},x)\times\mathscr{M}(x,x^{+}).

Thus we can define the
Morse–Bott homology of
f
by

\HM_{k}(f)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{\ker\partial_{k}}{\im\partial_{k+1}},\hskip 22.99988pt\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}.

As our notation suggests,
\HM(f)
is independent of any auxiliary data up to natural isomorphisms. In particular, as every Morse function is a Morse–Bott function, Morse–Bott homology is canonically isomorphic to the ordinary Morse homology of
M
, and thus to the singular homology of
M
with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, that is,

\HM_{*}(f)\cong\operatorname{H}_{*}(M;\mathbb{Z}_{2}).

Example 2.2 (
\mathbb{S}^{2n-1}
).

Consider the odd-dimensional sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2n-1}
with Morse–Bott function
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2n-1})
defined in Example
2.1
. Choose the standard height function
h\in C^{\infty}(\Crit f)
on each critical component
\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Then the associated chain complex is given by

\CM_{k}(f)\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}_{2}&0\leq k\leq 2n-1,\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

with boundary operator

\partial_{k}=\begin{cases}1&k\text{ even and }1\leq k\leq 2n-1,\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

See Figure
2
. Thus the resulting homology is

\HM_{k}(f)\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}_{2}&k=0,2n-1,\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

Figure 2
:
The sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2n-1}
with standard height function is depicted on the left and on the right we see the critical submanifold
\Crit f
with standard height function on each critical component.

Example 2.3 (The Teapot).

Consider the deformed sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
as in Figure
3
. Then the standard height function is a Morse–Bott function on the teapot with critical manifold being the disjoint union of a circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}
and four nondegenerate critical points. Choose also the standard height function on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Then the resulting chain complex is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}1\>\>1\\
0\>\>0\end{pmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}0\>\>1\\
0\>\>1\end{pmatrix}}

Thus the homology coincides again with
\operatorname{H}_{*}(\mathbb{S}^{2};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.

Figure 3
:
The deformed sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
resembling a teapot with the standard height function.

2.2
Hamiltonian Dynamics

The modern language of classical mechanics is provided by symplectic geometry. For an introduction to symplectic geometry see
[
Sil08
]
and for a more sophisticated treatment
[
MS17
]
. For an introduction to Hamiltonian dynamics see
[
AM78
]
as well as
[
HZ94
]
for a view towards symplectic invariants. Here we just briefly review the basics needed later on and to fix our conventions.

Definition 2.2 (Hamiltonian System).

A
Hamiltonian system
is a symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
, called the
phase space
together with a smooth function
H\in C^{\infty}(M)
, called a
Hamiltonian function
. We write
(M,\omega,H)
for a Hamiltonian system.

Definition 2.3 (Hamiltonian Vector Field).

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a Hamiltonian system. The
Hamiltonian vector field
is defined to be the vector field
X_{H}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
given implicitly by

i_{X_{H}}\omega=-dH.

Lemma 2.1 (Jacobi,
[
AM78
, Theorem 3.3.19]
).

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a Hamiltonian system and let
\varphi\in\Symp(M,\omega)
be a symplectomorphism. Then

\varphi^{*}X_{H}=X_{\varphi^{*}H}.

Proof.

We compute

\displaystyle i_{X_{\varphi^{*}H}}\omega=-d\varphi^{*}H=-\varphi^{*}dH=\varphi^{*}(i_{X_{H}}\omega)=i_{\varphi^{*}X_{H}}(\varphi^{*}\omega)=i_{\varphi^{*}X_{H}}\omega.

Thus we conclude by the uniqueness of the Hamiltonian vector field. ∎

Lemma 2.2.

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a Hamiltonian system and let
\varphi\in\Symp(M,\omega)
be a symplectomorphism. Then

\phi^{X_{\varphi^{*}H}}_{t}=\varphi^{-1}\circ\phi_{t}^{X_{H}}\circ\varphi,

whenever either side is defined, where
\phi
denotes the smooth flow of a vector field.

Proof.

Using Lemma
2.1
we compute

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\varphi^{-1}\circ\phi_{t}^{X_{H}}\circ\varphi

\displaystyle=D\varphi^{-1}\circ\frac{d}{dt}\phi^{X_{H}}_{t}\circ\varphi

\displaystyle=D\varphi^{-1}\circ X_{H}\circ\phi_{t}^{X_{H}}\circ\varphi

\displaystyle=D\varphi^{-1}\circ X_{H}\circ\varphi\circ\varphi^{-1}\circ\phi_{t}^{X_{H}}\circ\varphi

\displaystyle=\varphi^{*}X_{H}\circ\varphi^{-1}\circ\phi_{t}^{X_{H}}\circ\varphi

\displaystyle=X_{\varphi^{*}H}\circ\varphi^{-1}\circ\phi_{t}^{X_{H}}\circ\varphi,

and the result follows by the uniqueness of integral curves.
∎

Definition 2.4 (Algebra of Classical Observables,
[
Tak08
, p. 46]
).

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold. The commutative real algebra
C^{\infty}(M)
of smooth functions on
M
is called the
algebra of classical observables
.

Definition 2.5 (Poisson Bracket,
[
Lee12
, p. 578]
).

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold. Define a mapping, called the
Poisson bracket on the algebra of classical observables
,

\mathinner{\left\{\cdot,\cdot\right\}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}C^{\infty}(M)\times C^{\infty}(M)\to C^{\infty}(M)

by

\mathinner{\left\{f,g\right\}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\omega(X_{f},X_{g}).

Remark 2.2 (
[
Lee12
, Corollary 22.20]
).

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold. Then

(C^{\infty}(M),\{\cdot,\cdot\})\to(\mathfrak{X}(M),[\cdot,\cdot]),\hskip 22.99988ptf\mapsto X_{f},

is a Lie algebra homomorphism, where
[\cdot,\cdot]
denotes the Lie bracket given by

[X,Y]=L_{X}Y=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\mathinner{\bigl(\phi^{X}_{t}\bigr)}^{*}Y

for all
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
.

Lemma 2.3 (Evolution Equation,
[
AM78
, Corollary 3.3.15]
).

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a Hamiltonian system. Then

\frac{d}{dt}f\circ\phi^{X_{H}}_{t}=\{H,f\}\circ\phi^{X_{H}}_{t}\hskip 22.99988pt\forall f\in C^{\infty}(M),

whenever either side is defined.

Proof.

Using Fisherman’s formula
[
Lee12
, Proposition 22.14]
we compute

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}f\circ\phi^{X_{H}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\mathinner{\bigl(\phi_{t}^{X_{H}}\bigr)}^{*}f

\displaystyle=\mathinner{\bigl(\phi_{t}^{X_{H}}\bigr)}^{*}L_{X_{H}}f

\displaystyle=\mathinner{\bigl(\phi_{t}^{X_{H}}\bigr)}^{*}\{H,f\}

\displaystyle=\{H,f\}\circ\phi^{X_{H}}_{t}

for all
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
.
∎

Corollary 2.1 (Preservation of Energy,
[
FK18
, Theorem 2.2.2]
).

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a Hamiltonian system. Then

H\mathinner{\bigl(\phi^{X_{H}}_{t}(x)\bigr)}=H(x)\hskip 22.99988pt\forall x\in M,

whenever the left side is defined.

Proof.

Using Lemma
2.3
we compute

\frac{d}{dt}H\circ\phi^{X_{H}}_{t}=\{H,H\}\circ\phi^{X_{H}}_{t}=0

by antisymmetry of the Poisson bracket.
∎

We describe a particularly interesting class of Hamiltonian systems. Let
(M^{n},g)
be a compact Riemannian manifold and denote by
\pi\colon T^{*}M\to M
its cotangent bundle. For a smooth potential function
V\in C^{\infty}(M)
define
H\in C^{\infty}(T^{*}M)
by

H(q,p)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\lVert p\rVert}^{2}_{g^{*}}+V(q).

(2)

For
\sigma\in\Omega^{2}(M)
closed, the form
\omega_{\sigma}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=dp\wedge dq+\pi^{*}\sigma
is a symplectic form on
T^{*}M
where
(q,p)
denote the standard coordinates on the cotangent bundle. The symplectic manifold
(T^{*}M,\omega_{\sigma})
is called a
magnetic cotangent bundle
and the Hamiltonian system
(T^{*}M,\omega_{\sigma},H)
is called a
magnetic Hamiltonian system
. If
\sigma=0
, the system is called a
mechanical Hamiltonian system
. The dynamics of a magnetic Hamiltonian system are given by the flow of the associated Hamiltonian vector field

X_{H}(q,p)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial H}{\partial p_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{i}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\Biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{ij}(q)\frac{\partial H}{\partial p_{j}}-\frac{\partial H}{\partial q_{i}}\Biggr)}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{i}},

(3)

where
\sigma
is locally given by

\sigma=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\sigma_{ij}(q)dq_{i}\wedge dq_{j},\hskip 22.99988pt\sigma_{ji}=-\sigma_{ij}.

Assume that
\sigma
is exact, that is, there exists
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(M)
with
\sigma=d\lambda
. We claim that

\varphi_{\sigma}\colon(T^{*}M,\lambda_{T^{*}M}+\pi^{*}\lambda)\to(T^{*}M,\lambda_{T^{*}M}),\hskip 11.49994pt\varphi_{\sigma}(q,p)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(q,p+\lambda_{q})

is an exact symplectomorphism, where
\lambda_{T^{*}M}
denotes the canonical Liouville form on
T^{*}M
. For
(q,p)\in T^{*}M
and
v\in TT^{*}_{(q,p)}M
we compute

\displaystyle(\varphi^{*}_{\sigma}\lambda_{T^{*}M})_{(q,p)}(v)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\sigma}(q,p)(D\pi\circ D\varphi_{\sigma}(v))

\displaystyle=(p+\lambda_{q})(D(\pi\circ\varphi_{\sigma})(v))

\displaystyle=p(D\pi(v))+\lambda_{q}(D\pi(v))

\displaystyle=\lambda_{T^{*}M}|_{(q,p)}(v)+(\pi^{*}\lambda)_{(q,p)}(v).

The mechanical Hamiltonian (
2
) is transformed to the
magnetic Hamiltonian

H\circ\varphi^{-1}_{\sigma}(q,p)=\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\lVert p-\lambda_{q}\rVert}^{2}_{g^{*}}+V(q).

Definition 2.6 (Cotangent Lift).

Let
\varphi\in\Diff(M)
be a diffeomorphism of a smooth manifold
M
. Define a map
D\varphi^{\dagger}\colon T^{*}M\to T^{*}M
, called the
cotangent lift of the diffeomorphism
\varphi
, by

D\varphi^{\dagger}(q,p)(v)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=p\mathinner{\bigl(D\varphi^{-1}(v)\bigr)},\hskip 22.99988pt\forall v\in T_{\varphi(q)}M.

Proposition 2.1 (Physical Transformation,
[
FK18
, p. 10]
).

Let
\varphi\in\Diff(M)
be a diffeomorphism and denote by
\lambda_{T^{*}M}
the Liouville form on
T^{*}M
. Then

(D\varphi^{\dagger})^{*}\lambda_{T^{*}M}=\lambda_{T^{*}M}.

Proof.

Let
(q,p)\in T^{*}M
. Then we compute

\displaystyle(D\varphi^{\dagger})^{*}\lambda_{T^{*}M}|_{(q,p)}(v)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{T^{*}M}|_{D\varphi^{\dagger}(q,p)}\mathinner{\bigl(D(D\varphi^{\dagger})(v)\bigr)}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{T^{*}M}|_{(\varphi(q),p\circ D\varphi^{-1})}\mathinner{\bigl(D(D\varphi^{\dagger})(v)\bigr)}

\displaystyle=(p\circ D\varphi^{-1})\mathinner{\bigl(D\pi_{(\varphi(q),p\circ D\varphi^{-1})}\mathinner{\bigl(D(D\varphi^{\dagger})(v)\bigr)}\bigr)}

\displaystyle=p\mathinner{\bigl(D\varphi^{-1}\circ D(\pi\circ D\varphi^{\dagger})(v)\bigr)}

\displaystyle=p\mathinner{\bigl(D\varphi^{-1}\circ D(\varphi\circ\pi)(v)\bigr)}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{T^{*}M}|_{(q,p)}(v)

for all
v\in T_{(q,p)}T^{*}M
. ∎

Example 2.4 (Holomorphic Function).

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{C}
be an open subset and suppose that
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(U,\mathbb{C})
is holomorphic with
\varphi^{\prime}\neq 0
on
U
for the complex derivative
\varphi^{\prime}
of
\varphi
. Then the cotangent lift
D\varphi^{\dagger}
of
\varphi
is given by

D\varphi^{\dagger}\colon T^{*}U\to T^{*}\mathbb{C},\hskip 22.99988ptD\varphi^{\dagger}(z,w)=\mathinner{\biggl(\varphi(z),\frac{w}{\overline{\varphi^{\prime}(z)}}\biggr)}.

2.3
Morse–Bott Homology for the Symplectic Action Functional

In this section we briefly describe how to construct a Morse–Bott homology in a semi-infinite dimensional setting following
[
FN20
]
.

Definition 2.7 (The Symplectic Action Functional,
[
MS17
, p. 446]
).

Let
(M,\omega)
be a connected symplectic manifold such that
[\omega]|_{\pi_{2}(M)}=0
and denote by
\Lambda M
the connected component of contractible loops in
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},M)
. For
H\in C^{\infty}(M\times\mathbb{T})
, define the
symplectic action functional

\mathscr{A}_{H}\colon\Lambda M\to\mathbb{R},\hskip 22.99988pt\mathscr{A}_{H}(\gamma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathbb{D}}\overline{\gamma}^{*}\omega-\int_{0}^{1}H_{t}(\gamma(t))dt,

(4)

where
\overline{\gamma}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},M)
is a filling of
\gamma
, that is,
\overline{\gamma}(e^{2\pi it})=\gamma(t)
for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
.

The gradient
\grad_{J}\mathscr{A}_{H}
of the symplectic action functional is given by

\grad_{J}\mathscr{A}_{H}|_{\gamma}(t)=J(\dot{\gamma}(t)-X_{H_{t}}(\gamma(t)))\hskip 22.99988pt\forall t\in\mathbb{T},

for all
\gamma\in\Lambda M
and for some
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
J
on
(M,\omega)
with respect to the
L^{2}
-metric

\langle X,Y\rangle_{J}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{0}^{1}\omega(JX(t),Y(t))dt

for all
X,Y\in\Gamma(\gamma^{*}TM)
. Thus a negative gradient flow line of the symplectic action functional
\mathscr{A}_{H}
is a map
u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T},M)
that is a solution of the
Floer equation

\partial_{s}u(s,t)+J(\partial_{t}u(s,t)-X_{H_{t}}(u(s,t)))=0\hskip 22.99988pt\forall(s,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}.

(5)

Assume that
(M,\omega)
is compact and we are given a sequence
(u_{k})
of negative gradient flow lines of the symplectic action functional such that the derivatives
Du_{k}
are uniformly bounded. Then by
[
MS12
, Theorem 4.1.1]
there exists a negative gradient flow line
u
of the symplectic action functional such that

u_{k}\xrightarrow{C^{\infty}_{\loc}}u,\hskip 22.99988ptk\to\infty,

up to a subsequence. Thus if the derivatives of the sequence of negative gradient flow lines are uniformly bounded,
(u_{k})
converges to a broken negative gradient flow line in the Floer–Gromov sense. In contrast to finite-dimensional Morse–Bott homology, a new phenomenon occurs if the derivatives explode. Indeed, if the derivatives explode, by Theorem
D.1
there exists a nonconstant
J
-holomorphic sphere
v\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2},M)
, see Figure
4
. This cannot happen in our setting as we assumed
[\omega]|_{\pi_{2}(M)}=0
, but by
[
MS12
, Lemma 2.2.1]

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}v^{*}\omega>0.

Figure 4
:
The bubbling phenomenon of a sequence of solutions of the Floer equation.

By adapting Theorem
C.1
to the semi-infinite dimensional case as sketched in
[
Cie18
, Corollary 8.13]
, we can define the
Floer Homology of
H
by

\HF(H)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\HM(\mathscr{A}_{H})

if the symplectic action functional
\mathscr{A}_{H}
is a Morse–Bott function. More precisely, choose an additonal Morse function
h\in C^{\infty}(\Crit\mathscr{A}_{H})
on
\Crit\mathscr{A}_{H}\subseteq M
via the obvious identification
\gamma\mapsto\gamma(0)
. Define a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-vector space
\CF(H)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Crit h\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and a boundary operator

\partial\colon\CF(\mathscr{A}_{H})\to\CF(\mathscr{A}_{H}),\hskip 22.99988pt\partial\gamma^{-}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{\gamma^{+}\in\Crit h}n(\gamma^{-},\gamma^{+})\gamma^{+},

where

n(\gamma^{-},\gamma^{+})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\#_{2}\mathscr{M}^{0}(\gamma^{-},\gamma^{+})\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}

denotes the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-count of the zero dimensional component of the moduli space of all abstractly perturbed unpartametrised negative gradient flow lines with cascades. Then the ungraded Floer homology of
H\in C^{\infty}(M\times\mathbb{T})
is given by

\HF(H)=\frac{\ker\partial}{\im\partial}.

Again, one can show that the definition of Hamiltonian Floer homology does not depend on any auxiliary choices. In fact, Hamiltonian Floer homology is also independent of the choice of time-dependent Hamiltonian function
H
. Consequently, we have a chain of natural isomorphisms

\HF(H)=\HM(\mathscr{A}_{H})\cong\HM(\mathscr{A}_{0})=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}\HM_{k}(h)\cong\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}\operatorname{H}_{k}(M;\mathbb{Z}_{2}).

(6)

Consequently, we have that

\#\Crit\mathscr{A}_{H}=\dim_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\CF(H)\geq\dim_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\HF(H)=\sum_{k=0}^{\dim M}\dim_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\operatorname{H}_{k}(M;\mathbb{Z}_{2})

This resembles the famous Morse inequalities and yields a proof of a special case for the following conjecture.

Conjecture 2.1 (Homological Arnold Conjecture).

Let
(M,\omega)
be a compact symplectic manifold and
H\in C^{\infty}(M\times\mathbb{T})
such that
\mathscr{A}_{H}
is a Morse function. Then the number of contractible periodic orbits
\mathscr{P}(H)
of
H
satisfies the inequality

\#\mathscr{P}(H)\geq\sum_{k=0}^{\dim M}\dim_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\operatorname{H}_{k}(M;\mathbb{Z}_{2}).

For a discussion of the general homological Arnold conjecture, see
[
AS19
, p. 29]
.

We finally discuss a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading for Hamiltonian Floer homology. There is an obvious
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading, but the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading requires an additional assumption. First, we observe that the ordinary Morse index and coindex are both infinite for the symplectic action functional. Indeed, as
\mathscr{A}_{H}
is a zero-order perturbation of the symplectic area functional
\mathscr{A}_{0}
, it is enough to consider that case. Using a Darboux chart, it is actually enough to consider a loop
z\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n})
. This loop can be represented by its Fourier series

z=\sum_{k=-\infty}^{+\infty}z_{k}e^{2\pi ikt},\hskip 22.99988ptz_{k}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}.

Then

\mathscr{A}_{0}(z)=-\pi\sum_{k=-\infty}^{+\infty}k\mathinner{\lVert z_{k}\rVert}^{2}=\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathscr{A}(z).

(7)

Indeed, we have that
\mathscr{A}_{0}(0)=0=\mathscr{A}(0)
and as
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n})
is connected, it suffices to show that the differential on both sides of (
7
) coincide. Represent the tangent vector
v\in T_{z}C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n})\cong C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n})
by its Fourier series as

v=\sum_{k=-\infty}^{+\infty}v_{k}e^{2\pi ikt},\hskip 22.99988ptv_{k}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}.

Then we compute

\displaystyle d\mathscr{A}_{0}|_{z}(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\omega(v(t),\dot{z}(t))dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\operatorname{Re}(i\overline{v}(t)\dot{z}(t))dt

\displaystyle=-2\pi\operatorname{Re}\mathinner{\Biggl(\sum_{k=-\infty}^{+\infty}k\overline{v}_{k}z_{k}\Biggr)}

\displaystyle=-\pi\sum_{k=-\infty}^{+\infty}k(\overline{v}_{k}z_{k}+v_{k}\overline{z}_{k})

\displaystyle=d\mathscr{A}|_{z}(v).

Thus the gradient
\grad\mathscr{A}_{0}
with respect to the standard real inner product on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
is given by

\grad\mathscr{A}_{0}|_{z}^{k}=-2\pi kz_{k}\hskip 22.99988pt\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}.

Consequently, the spectrum of the Hessian of
\mathscr{A}_{0}
is
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
and we see that
\mathscr{A}_{0}
has infinite Morse index and coindex. Let
\gamma\in\Crit\mathscr{A}_{H}
be a critical point of the symplectic action functional and choose a filling disk
\overline{\gamma}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},M^{2n})
of
\gamma
. Fix a symplectic trivialisation of the pullback tangent bundle
\Phi\colon\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}\to\overline{\gamma}^{*}TM
. This is possible by
[
MS17
, Proposition 2.6.7]
. Then we can associate to the pair
(\gamma,\overline{\gamma})
an integer by

\mu(\gamma,\overline{\gamma})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mu_{\CZ}(\Psi)\in\mathbb{Z},

where
\mu_{\CZ}
denotes the Conley–Zehnder index
[
FK18
, Definition 10.4.1]
of the path of symplectic matrices
\Psi
defined by

\Psi\colon\mathinner{[0,1]}\to\Sp(n),\hskip 22.99988pt\Psi(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Phi^{-1}_{e^{2\pi it}}\circ D\phi^{X_{H_{t}}}_{t}\circ\Phi_{1}.

If we choose another filling disk
\overline{\gamma}^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},M)
of
\gamma
, then we have the formula

\mu(\gamma,\overline{\gamma})-\mu(\gamma,\overline{\gamma}^{\prime})=2c_{1}([\overline{\gamma}^{\prime}\#\overline{\gamma}^{-}]),

where
[\overline{\gamma}^{\prime}\#\overline{\gamma}^{-}]\in\pi_{2}(M)
and
c_{1}
denotes the first Chern class
[
MS17
, p. 85]
. So there is always a well-defined
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading of
\CF(H)
. If
c_{1}(TM)|_{\pi_{2}(M)}=0
, there is also a well-defined
\mathbb{Z}
-grading. More precisely, if
c_{1}(TM)|_{\pi_{2}(M)}=0
, then the Floer chain group
\CF(H)
is graded by the signature index

\mu_{\CZ}(\gamma)-\frac{1}{2}\sgn\Hess h(\gamma)\hskip 22.99988pt\forall\gamma\in\Crit h.

See
[
CF09
, p. 297]
. Note that the chain of canonical isomorphisms (
6
) gives rise to a natural isomorphism
\HF_{*}(M)\cong\operatorname{H}_{\ast+n}(M;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.

2.4
Regular Energy Surfaces

In contrast to Floer homology, in Rabinowitz–Floer homology, we study an arbitrary period but fixed energy problem. Thus we need to consider hypersurfaces in Hamiltonian systems.

Definition 2.8 (Regular Energy Surface).

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a Hamiltonian system. The level set
\Sigma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=H^{-1}(0)
is a
regular energy surface
, if we have that
\Crit H\cap\Sigma=\varnothing
.

Definition 2.9 (Hamiltonian Manifold,
[
FK18
, Definition 2.4.1]
).

A
Hamiltonian manifold
is defined to be a pair
(\Sigma,\omega)
, where
\Sigma
is an odd-dimensional smooth manifold and
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(\Sigma)
is closed such that
\ker\omega
is a line distribution. The foliation inducing the line distribution
\ker\omega
is called the
characteristic foliation
.

Example 2.5 (Regular Energy Surface).

Let
\Sigma
be a regular energy surface in a Hamiltonian system
(M,\omega,H)
. Then
(\Sigma,\omega|_{\Sigma})
is a Hamiltonian manifold. Moreover, the line distribution
\ker\omega|_{\Sigma}
is spanned by the Hamiltonian vector field
X_{H}|_{\Sigma}
.

Definition 2.10 (Stable Hamiltonian Manifold,
[
CFP10
, p. 1773]
).

A Hamiltonian manifold
(\Sigma,\omega)
is called
stable
, if there exists
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)
which is nowhere-vanishing on
\ker\omega
and such that
\ker\omega\subseteq\ker d\lambda
. We write
(\Sigma,\omega,\lambda)
for a stable Hamiltonian manifold.

Remark 2.3.

Equivalently, a Hamiltonian manifold
(\Sigma^{2n-1},\omega)
is stable, if and only if there exists
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)
with
\ker\omega\subseteq\ker d\lambda
and such that
\lambda\wedge\omega^{n-1}
is a volume form on
\Sigma
.

Example 2.6 (Regular Energy Surface).

Let
\Sigma
be a regular energy surface in a Hamiltonian system
(M,\omega,H)
. Suppose that there exists a vector field
X
in a neighbourhood of
\Sigma
with
X
nowhere-tangent to
\Sigma
and
\ker\omega|_{\Sigma}\subseteq\ker L_{X}\omega|_{\Sigma}
. Then
(\Sigma,\omega|_{\Sigma},i_{X}\omega|_{\Sigma})
is a stable Hamiltonian manifold.

Example 2.7 (
[
CFP10
, Section 6.1]
).

Let
\mathbb{T}^{n}
be the standard flat torus for
n\geq 2
and let
J\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an antisymmetric nonzero linear map. Define
\sigma\in\Omega^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n})
by setting
\sigma(\cdot,\cdot)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\langle\cdot,J\cdot\rangle
and denote by
\omega_{\sigma}=dp\wedge dq+\pi^{*}\sigma
the magnetic symplectic form on
T^{*}\mathbb{T}^{n}\cong\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For an energy value
c\in\mathbb{R}
set
\Sigma_{c}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=H^{-1}(c)
for the mechanical Hamiltonian function

H(q,p)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\lVert p\rVert}^{2}\hskip 22.99988pt\forall(q,p)\in\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Define
A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(J|_{\im J})^{-1}
and
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(\im J)
by

\alpha_{x}(v)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{2}\langle x,Av\rangle.

Then
\Sigma_{c}
is a stable Hamiltonian manifold for every
c>0
by
[
CFP10
, Proposition 6.3]
. The stabilising form
\lambda
on
\Sigma_{c}
is given by

\lambda\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=f^{*}(pdq)+(\pr_{\parallel}\circ\pr)^{*}\alpha,

(8)

where

\pr_{\perp}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\ker J,\hskip 22.99988pt\pr_{\parallel}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\im J,\hskip 22.99988pt\pr\colon\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}

denote the projections with respect to the orthogonal splitting

\mathbb{R}^{n}=\ker J\oplus\im J,

and

f\colon\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\hskip 22.99988ptf(q,p)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\bigl(q,\pr_{\perp}(p)\bigr)}.

Definition 2.11 (Reeb Vector Field,
[
CFP10
, p. 1773]
).

Let
(\Sigma,\omega,\lambda)
be a stable Hamiltonian manifold. The unique vector field
R\in\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma)
implicitly defined by

i_{R}\omega=0\hskip 22.99988pt\text{and}\hskip 22.99988pti_{R}\lambda=1

is called the
Reeb vector field
.

Example 2.8.

The flow
\phi_{t}
of the magnetic Hamiltonian system in Example
2.7
is given by

\phi_{t}(q,p)=\mathinner{\biggl(\int_{0}^{t}e^{sJ}pds+q,e^{tJ}p\biggr)},

as one can explicitly compute this flow using (
3
), and
(q,p)\in\Sigma_{c}
gives rise to a contractible closed orbit of period
\tau
if and only if

\pr_{\perp}(p)=0,\hskip 22.99988pte^{\tau J}\pr_{\parallel}(p)=\pr_{\parallel}(p),\hskip 22.99988pt\text{and}\hskip 22.99988pt\mathinner{\!\bigl\lVert\pr_{\parallel}(p)\bigr\rVert}^{2}=2c.

(9)

It is illustrative to consider the special case
n=2
and
\sigma=dq_{1}\wedge dq_{2}
. Then

\lambda=-\frac{1}{2}(p_{1}dp_{2}-p_{2}dp_{1}),

and the projection of a contractible periodic orbit to
\mathbb{T}^{2}
is depicted in Figure
5
.

Figure 5
:
A contractible periodic Reeb orbit on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
.

The following provides a large class of examples of stable Hamiltonian manifolds.

Definition 2.12 (Contact Manifold,
[
FK18
, Definition 2.5.1]
).

A
contact manifold
is defined to be a stable Hamiltonian manifold
(\Sigma,d\lambda,\lambda)
. We simply write
(\Sigma,\lambda)
for a contact manifold.

Example 2.9 (Regular Energy Surface,
[
AH19
, Theorem 1.2.2]
).

Let
\Sigma
be a compact regular energy surface in a mechanical Hamiltonian system
(T^{*}M,\omega_{0},H)
. Then there exists
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)
such that
d\lambda=\omega|_{\Sigma}
and
(\Sigma,\lambda)
is a contact manifold.

Definition 2.13 (Liouville Domain,
[
FK18
, Definition 2.6.2]
).

A
Liouville domain
is a compact connected exact symplectic manifold
(W,\lambda)
with connected boundary such that the Liouville vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(W)
, implicitly defined by
i_{X}d\lambda=\lambda
, is outward-pointing along the boundary
\partial W
.

Remark 2.4 (
[
FK18
, p. 24]
).

For a Liouville domain
(W^{2n},\lambda)
we have that
\partial W\neq\varnothing
. Indeed, if
\partial W=\varnothing
, then using Stokes Theorem we compute

0<\int_{W}d\lambda^{n}=\int_{W}d(\lambda\wedge d\lambda^{n-1})=\int_{\partial W}\lambda\wedge d\lambda^{n-1}=0.

Remark 2.5 (
[
FK18
, Lemma 2.63]
).

Let
(W,\lambda)
be a Liouville domain. Then the boundary
(\partial W,\lambda|_{\partial W})
is a contact manifold.

Remark 2.6.

In our definition of a Liouville domain
(W,\lambda)
it would actually suffice to assume that the Liouville vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(W)
is nowhere-tangent to the boundary
\Sigma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\partial W
. Indeed, if the Liouville vector field is inward-pointing at the boundary, we get an exact symplectic embedding

\psi\colon\mathinner{\bigl(\mathinner{[0,+\infty)}\times\Sigma,e^{r}\lambda|_{\Sigma}\bigr)}\hookrightarrow(W,\lambda)

defined by

\psi(r,x)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\phi^{X}_{t}(x),

where
\phi^{X}
denotes the smooth flow of
X
. But
\psi
expands volume as
\psi^{*}_{r}\lambda=e^{r}\lambda|_{\Sigma}
.

Example 2.10 (Star-Shaped Domain,
[
FK18
, Example 2.6.6]
).

Denote by
(\mathbb{C}^{n},\lambda)
the standard exact linear symplectic manifold where

\lambda\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathinner{\bigl(y_{j}dx_{j}-x_{j}dy_{j}\bigr)}=\frac{i}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathinner{\bigl(\overline{z}_{j}dz_{j}-z_{j}d\overline{z}_{j}\bigr)},

(10)

with coordinates
z_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=x_{j}+iy_{j}
. Then the Liouville vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
is given by

X=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathinner{\biggl(x_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}+y_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{j}}\biggr)}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathinner{\biggl(z_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}+\overline{z}_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{j}}\biggr)}.

(11)

If
U\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}
is a bounded connected open subset which is star-shaped with respect to the origin and with a smooth connected boundary
\partial U
, then
(\overline{U},\lambda|_{\overline{U}})
is a Liouville domain. All star-shaped Liouvile domains are diffeomorphic to
(B^{n},\lambda|_{B^{n}})
via the radial projection, where
B^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathinner{\lVert z\rVert}\leq 1\}
denotes the closed unit disc. However, the Reeb flow of these star-shaped contact type boundaries can be very different as in
[
AS19
, Example 5.1]
.

Example 2.11 (Cotangent Bundle,
[
AS19
, Example 5.2]
).

Let
(M,g)
be a compact connected Riemannian manifold and consider the cotangent bundle
(T^{*}M,pdq)
. Then the Liouville vector field is locally given by
p\frac{\partial}{\partial p}
. Suppose
U\subseteq T^{*}M
is a bounded connected open set with smooth boundary containing the zero section and such that the fibrewise intersection
U\cap T^{*}_{q}M
is star-shaped with respect to the origin for all
q\in M
. Then
(\overline{U},pdq|_{\overline{U}})
is a Liouville domain. Any such star-shaped Liouville domain in the cotangent bundle
T^{*}M
is diffeomorphic to the
unit cotangent bundle

D^{*}M\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{(q,p)\in T^{*}M\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathinner{\lVert p\rVert}_{g^{*}}\leq 1\},

with contact type boundary the spherisation
(S^{*}M,pdq|_{S^{*}M})
. Once more, the Reeb flow of such star-shaped hypersurfaces can be very different.

Example 2.12 (Magnetic Hamiltonian System,
[
AS19
, Example 5.2]
).

Consider an exact magnetic Hamiltonian system
(T^{*}M,pdq+\pi^{*}\lambda,H)
for
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(M)
.
For

c>\max_{q\in M}\mathinner{\biggl(\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\lVert\lambda_{q}\rVert}^{2}_{m^{*}}+V(q)\biggr)}

the level set
H^{-1}(c)
is fibrewise star-shaped.

Definition 2.14 (Liouville Automorphism,
[
CE12
, p. 237]
).

Let
(W,\lambda)
be a Liouville domain with boundary
\Sigma
. A diffeomorphism
\varphi\in\Diff(W)
is said to be a
Liouville automorphism
, if
\varphi^{*}\lambda-\lambda
is exact and compactly supported in the interior
\Int W
, and
\ord\varphi<\infty
. We denote by
\Aut(W,\lambda)
the set of all Liouville automorphisms on a given Liouville domain
(W,\lambda)
.

Remark 2.7.

Let
\varphi\in\Aut(W,\lambda)
be a Liouville automorphism. Then there exists a unique function
f_{\varphi}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Int W)
such that
\varphi^{*}\lambda-\lambda=df_{\varphi}
.

Remark 2.8.

For a Liouville domain
(W,\lambda)
, the set
\Aut(W,\lambda)
of Liouville automorphisms is in general not a group. Indeed, for
\varphi,\psi\in\Aut(W,\lambda)
it is not necessarily true that
\varphi\circ\psi
is of finite order unless
\varphi
and
\psi
commute.

Remark 2.9.

Any
\varphi\in\Aut(W,\lambda)
induces a strict contactomorphism
\varphi|_{\partial W}
.

Example 2.13 (Rotation).

For
m\geq 1
consider the rotation

\varphi\colon\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}^{n},\hskip 22.99988pt\varphi(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\bigl(e^{2\pi ik_{1}/m}z_{1},\dots,e^{2\pi ik_{n}/m}z_{n}\bigr)},

where
k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
are coprime to
m
. Let
(W,\lambda)
be a star-shaped Liouville domain in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
as in Example
2.10
invariant under the rotation
\varphi
, that is,
\varphi(\partial W)=\partial W
. Then
\varphi
is a Liouville automorphism as
\varphi^{*}\lambda=\lambda
by (
10
) and
\ord\varphi=m
.

Figure 6
:
The completion of a Liouville domain.

One can complete a Liouville domain
(W,\lambda)
to a noncompact exact symplectic manifold without boundary by attaching the positive cylindrical end
\mathinner{[0,+\infty)}\times\partial W
to its boundary
\partial W
. See Figure
6
.

Definition 2.15 (Completion of a Liouville Domain,
[
MS17
, p. 148]
).

Let
(W,\lambda)
be a Liouville domain with boundary
\Sigma
. The
completion of
(W,\lambda)
is defined to be the exact symplectic manifold
(M,\lambda)
, where

M\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=W\cup_{\Sigma}\mathinner{[0,+\infty)}\times\Sigma\hskip 22.99988pt\text{and}\hskip 22.99988pt\lambda|_{\mathinner{[0,+\infty)}\times\Sigma}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=e^{r}\lambda|_{\Sigma}.

Example 2.14 (Star-Shaped Domain).

Let
(W,\lambda)
be a star-shaped Liouville domain as in Example
2.10
. Then the completion
(M,\lambda)
of
(W,\lambda)
is symplectomorphic to the exact linear symplectic manifold
(\mathbb{C}^{n},\lambda)
via the flow of the Liouville vector field. See Figure
7
. The completion of a star-shaped Liouville domain in a cotangent bundle
T^{*}M
as in Example
2.11
is
(T^{*}M,pdq)
.

Finally, we consider more general hypersurfaces in symplectic manifolds.

Definition 2.16 (Stable Hypersurface,
[
CFP10
, p. 1774]
).

Let
(M,\omega)
be a connected symplectic manifold. A
stable hypersurface in
(M,\omega)
is a compact connected hypersurface
\Sigma\subseteq M
such that the following conditions are satisfied.

(i)

\Sigma
is separating, that is,
M\setminus\Sigma
consists of two connected components
M^{\pm}
, where
M^{-}
is bounded and
M^{+}
is unbounded.

(ii)

There exists a vector field
X
in a neighbourhood of
\Sigma
such that
X
is outward-pointing to
\Sigma\cup M^{-}
and such that
\ker\omega|_{\Sigma}\subseteq\ker L_{X}\omega|_{\Sigma}
.

Definition 2.17 (Displaceability,
[
MS17
, p. 411]
).

A subset
A\subseteq M
of a symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
is said to be
Hamiltonianly displaceable
, if there exists a compactly supported Hamiltonian symplectomorphism
\varphi_{F}\in\Ham_{c}(M,\omega)
, such that

\varphi_{F}(A)\cap A=\varnothing.

Example 2.15 (
[
FS07
, p. 4]
).

Every compact subset of
(M\times\mathbb{C},\omega\oplus\omega_{0})
is Hamiltonianly displaceable, where
(M,\omega)
is any symplectic manifold.

Figure 7
:
The completion
(\mathbb{C}^{n},\lambda)
of a star-shaped Liouville domain.

Definition 2.18 (Hofer Norm,
[
MS17
, p. 466]
).

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold and
F\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times\mathinner{[0,1]})
. Define the
Hofer norm of
F
by

\mathinner{\!\left\lVert F\right\rVert}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\!\left\lVert F\right\rVert}_{+}+\mathinner{\!\left\lVert F\right\rVert}_{-},

where

\mathinner{\!\left\lVert F\right\rVert}_{+}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{0}^{1}\max_{x\in M}F_{t}(x)dt\hskip 22.99988pt\text{and}\hskip 22.99988pt\mathinner{\!\left\lVert F\right\rVert}_{-}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=-\int_{0}^{1}\min_{x\in M}F_{t}(x)dt.

Definition 2.19 (Displacement Energy,
[
MS17
, p. 469]
).

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold and
A\subseteq M
a compact subset. The
displacement energy of
A
is

e(A)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times\mathinner{[0,1]})\\
\varphi_{F}(A)\cap A=\varnothing\end{subarray}}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert F\right\rVert}.

Example 2.16.

Consider the displaceable hypersurface
\Sigma_{c}\subseteq(T^{*}\mathbb{T}^{n},\omega_{\sigma},H)
as in Example
2.7
. Then by
[
MS17
, Theorem 12.3.4]
, the displacement energy of
\Sigma_{c}
is given by

e(\Sigma_{c})=e\mathinner{\bigl(\overline{B}^{n}_{\sqrt{2c}}(0)\bigr)}=2\pi c\hskip 22.99988pt\forall c>0,

where
\overline{B}^{n}_{\sqrt{2c}}(0)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
denotes the closed ball around the origin with radius
\sqrt{2c}
.

3
Twisted Rabinowitz–Floer Homology

In this chapter we construct the generalisation of Rabinowitz–Floer homology and prove Theorem
1.4
.

To begin, we define the twisted Rabinowitz action functional for an exact symplectic manifold and compute its first and second variation.

In the second section we prove a compactness result for the moduli space of twisted negative gradient flow lines in a restricted geometrical setting. This follows a standard procedure, but one has to carefully adapt the constructions and proofs to this more general case.

In the third section we define ungraded and graded twisted Rabinowitz–Floer homology and prove part (b) of Theorem
1.4
in Proposition
3.3
.

In the fourth section we briefly illustrate how to prove part (a) of Theorem
1.4
(see Theorem
3.2
) and define the notion of twisted homotopies of Liouville domains.

In the last section we prove an important vanishing result for twisted Rabinowitz–Floer homology, that is, part (c) of Theorem
1.4
(see Theorem
3.3
).

3.1
The Twisted Rabinowitz Action Functional

Definition 3.1 (Free Twisted Loop Space).

Let
\varphi\in\Diff(M)
be a diffeomorphism of a smooth manifold
M
. Define the
free twisted loop space of
M
and
\varphi
by

\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\{\gamma\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},M)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\gamma(t+1)=\varphi(\gamma(t))\>\forall t\in\mathbb{R}\}}.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold and let
\varphi\in\Symp(M,\omega)
. Given a twisted loop
\gamma\in\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M
and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, we say that a curve

\mathinner{(-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0})}\to\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M,\hskip 22.99988pt\varepsilon\mapsto\gamma_{\varepsilon}

starting at
\gamma
is
smooth
, if the induced variation

\mathbb{R}\times\mathinner{(-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0})}\to M,\hskip 22.99988pt(t,\varepsilon)\mapsto\gamma_{\varepsilon}(t)

is smooth. Since
\gamma_{\varepsilon}(t+1)=\varphi(\gamma_{\varepsilon}(t))
holds for all
\varepsilon\in\mathinner{(-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0})}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we call such a variation a
twisted variation
. Then the infinitesimal variation

\delta\gamma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{\partial\gamma_{\varepsilon}}{\partial\varepsilon}\bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}\in\mathfrak{X}(\gamma),

satisfies

\delta\gamma(t+1)=D\varphi(\delta\gamma(t))\hskip 22.99988pt\forall t\in\mathbb{R}.

Lemma 3.1.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold and let
\varphi\in\Symp(M,\omega)
be of finite order. Let
\gamma\in\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M
and let
X\in\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
be such that

X(t+1)=D\varphi(X(t))\hskip 22.99988pt\forall t\in\mathbb{R}.

Then there exists a twisted variation of
\gamma
such that
\delta\gamma=X
.

Proof.

As
\varphi
is assumed to be of finite order, there exists a
\varphi
-invariant
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
J
on
M
by
[
MS17
, Lemma 5.5.6]
. With respect to the induced Riemannian metric

m_{J}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\omega(J\cdot,\cdot),

the symplectomorphism
\varphi
is an isometry. Define the exponential variation

\mathbb{R}\times\mathinner{(-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0})}\to M,\hskip 22.99988pt\gamma_{\varepsilon}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\exp^{\nabla_{J}}_{\gamma(t)}(\varepsilon X(t)),

for
\varepsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small and
\nabla_{J}
denoting the Levi–Civita connection associated with
m_{J}
. Such an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
exists by the naturality of geodesics
[
Lee18
, Corollary 5.14]
. Then we compute

\displaystyle\gamma_{\varepsilon}(t+1)

\displaystyle=\exp^{\nabla_{J}}_{\gamma(t+1)}(\varepsilon X(t+1))

\displaystyle=\exp^{\nabla_{J}}_{\varphi(\gamma(t))}(D\varphi(\varepsilon X(t)))

\displaystyle=\varphi\mathinner{\bigl(\exp^{\nabla_{J}}_{\gamma(t)}(\varepsilon X(t))\bigr)}

\displaystyle=\varphi(\gamma_{\varepsilon}(t))

by naturality of the exponential map
[
Lee18
, Proposition 5.20]
.
∎

Remark 3.1.

The statement of Lemma
3.1
remains true if
\ord\varphi=\infty
.

This discussion together with Lemma
A.1
motivates the following definition of the tangent space to the free twisted loop space.

Definition 3.2 (Tangent Space to the Free Twisted Loop Space).

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold and
\varphi\in\Symp(M,\omega)
. For
\gamma\in\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M
define the
tangent space to the free twisted loop space at
\gamma
by

T_{\gamma}\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\{X\in\Gamma(\gamma^{*}TM)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X(t+1)=D\varphi(X(t))\>\forall t\in\mathbb{R}\}}.

Definition 3.3 (Twisted Hamiltonian Function).

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold and
\varphi\in\Symp(M,\omega)
. A function
H\in C^{\infty}(M\times\mathbb{R})
is said to be a
twisted Hamiltonian function
, if

\varphi^{*}H_{t+1}=H_{t}\hskip 22.99988pt\forall t\in\mathbb{R}.

We denote the space of all twisted Hamiltonian functions by
C^{\infty}_{\varphi}(M\times\mathbb{R})
and the subspace of all autonomous twisted Hamiltonian functions by
C^{\infty}_{\varphi}(M)
.

Recall, that an exact symplectic manifold is a pair
(M,\lambda)
such that
(M,d\lambda)
is a symplectic manifold. Moreover, an exact symplectomorphism of an exact symplectic manifold
(M,\lambda)
is a diffeomorphism
\varphi\in\Diff(M)
such that
\varphi^{*}\lambda-\lambda
is exact.

Definition 3.4 (Perturbed Twisted Rabinowitz Action Functional).

Let
(M,\lambda)
be an exact symplectic manifold and
\varphi\in\Diff(M)
an exact symplectomorphism such that
\varphi^{*}\lambda-\lambda=df
. For
H,F\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi}(M\times\mathbb{R})
define the
perturbed twisted Rabinowitz action functional

\mathscr{A}^{(H,F)}_{\varphi}\colon\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

by

\mathscr{A}^{(H,F)}_{\varphi}(\gamma,\tau)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{0}^{1}\gamma^{*}\lambda-\tau\int_{0}^{1}H_{t}(\gamma(t))dt-\int_{0}^{1}F_{t}(\gamma(t))dt-f(\gamma(0)).

If
F=0
and
H\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi}(M)
, we write
\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}
for
\mathscr{A}^{(H,F)}_{\varphi}
and call
\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}
the
twisted Rabinowitz action functional
.

Remark 3.2.

Assume that
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\ord\varphi<\infty
. Then

\mathscr{A}^{(H,F)}_{\varphi}(\gamma,\tau)=\frac{1}{m}\mathscr{A}^{(H,F)}(\overline{\gamma},\tau)-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}f(\gamma(k)),

for all
(\gamma,\tau)\in\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M
, where
\overline{\gamma}\in\mathscr{L}M
is defined by
\overline{\gamma}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\gamma(mt)
.

Definition 3.5 (Differential of the Perturbed Twisted Rabinowitz Action Functional).

Let
\varphi\in\Diff(M)
be an exact symplectomorphism of an exact symplectic manifold
(M,\lambda)
. For
H,F\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi}(M\times\mathbb{R})
, define the
differential of the perturbed twisted Rabinowitz action functional

d\mathscr{A}^{(H,F)}_{\varphi}|_{(\gamma,\tau)}\colon T_{\gamma}\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

for all
(\gamma,\tau)\in\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\times\mathbb{R}
by

d\mathscr{A}^{(H,F)}_{\varphi}|_{(\gamma,\tau)}(X,\eta)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}\mathscr{A}^{(H,F)}_{\varphi}(\gamma_{\varepsilon},\tau+\varepsilon\eta),

where
\gamma_{\varepsilon}
is a twisted variation of
\gamma
such that
\delta\gamma=X
.

Proposition 3.1 (Differential of the Perturbed Twisted Rabinowitz Action Functional).

Let
\varphi\in\Diff(M)
be an exact symplectomorphism of an exact symplectic manifold
(M,\lambda)
and
H,F\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi}(M\times\mathbb{R})
. Then

d\mathscr{\mathscr{A}}^{(H,F)}_{\varphi}|_{(\gamma,\tau)}(X,\eta)=\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda(X(t),\dot{\gamma}(t)-\tau X_{H_{t}}(\gamma(t))-X_{F_{t}}(\gamma(t)))dt\\
-\eta\int_{0}^{1}H_{t}(\gamma(t))dt

(12)

for all
(\gamma,\tau)\in\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\times\mathbb{R}
and
(X,\eta)\in T_{\gamma}\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\times\mathbb{R}
. Moreover,
(\gamma,\tau)\in\Crit\mathscr{A}^{(H,F)}_{\varphi}
if and only if

\dot{\gamma}(t)=\tau X_{H_{t}}(\gamma(t))+X_{F_{t}}(\gamma(t))\hskip 22.99988pt\text{and}\hskip 22.99988pt\int_{0}^{1}H_{t}(\gamma(t))dt=0

(13)

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

In order to show (
12
), we compute

\displaystyle d\mathscr{\mathscr{A}}^{(H,F)}_{\varphi}|_{(\gamma,\tau)}(X,\eta)=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}\mathscr{A}^{(H,F)}_{\varphi}(\gamma_{\varepsilon},\tau+\varepsilon\eta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\gamma^{*}i_{X}d\lambda+\int_{0}^{1}di_{X}\lambda-\tau\int_{0}^{1}dH_{t}(X(t))dt

\displaystyle-\eta\int_{0}^{1}H_{t}(\gamma(t))dt-\int_{0}^{1}dF_{t}(X(t))dt-df_{\varphi}(X(0))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda(X(t),\dot{\gamma}(t)-\tau X_{H_{t}}(\gamma(t))-X_{F_{t}}(\gamma(t)))dt

\displaystyle-\eta\int_{0}^{1}H_{t}(\gamma(t))dt+\lambda(X)|_{0}^{1}-df_{\varphi}(X(0))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda(X(t),\dot{\gamma}(t)-\tau X_{H_{t}}(\gamma(t))-X_{F_{t}}(\gamma(t)))dt

\displaystyle-\eta\int_{0}^{1}H_{t}(\gamma(t))dt+(\varphi^{*}\lambda-\lambda)(X(0))-df_{\varphi}(X(0))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda(X(t),\dot{\gamma}(t)-\tau X_{H_{t}}(\gamma(t))-X_{F_{t}}(\gamma(t)))dt

\displaystyle-\eta\int_{0}^{1}H_{t}(\gamma(t))dt.

Let
(\gamma,\tau)\in\Crit\mathscr{A}^{(H,F)}_{\varphi}
. It follows immediately from (
12
) that

\int_{0}^{1}H_{t}(\gamma(t))dt=0

and

\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda(X(t),\dot{\gamma}(t)-\tau X_{H_{t}}(\gamma(t))-X_{F_{t}}(\gamma(t)))dt=0

for all
X\in T_{\gamma}\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M
. Suppose there exists
t_{0}\in\Int I
such that

\dot{\gamma}(t_{0})-\tau X_{H_{t_{0}}}(\gamma(t_{0}))-X_{F_{t_{0}}}(\gamma(t_{0}))\neq 0.

By nondegeneracy of the symplectic form
d\lambda
there exists
v\in T_{\gamma(t_{0})}M
with

d\lambda(v,\dot{\gamma}(t_{0})-\tau X_{H_{t_{0}}}(\gamma(t_{0}))-X_{F_{t_{0}}}(\gamma(t_{0})))\neq 0.

Fix a Riemannian metric on
M
and let
X_{v}
denote the unique parallel vector field along
\gamma|_{I}
such that
X_{v}(t_{0})=v
. As
\Int I
is open, there exists
\delta>0
such that
\overline{B}_{\delta}(t_{0})\subseteq\Int I
. Fix a smooth bump function
\beta\in C^{\infty}(I)
for
t_{0}
supported in
B_{\delta}(t_{0})
. By shrinking
\delta
if necessary, we may assume that

\int_{t_{0}-\delta}^{t_{0}+\delta}d\lambda(\beta(t)X_{v}(t),\dot{\gamma}(t)-\tau X_{H_{t}}(\gamma(t))-X_{F_{t}}(\gamma(t)))dt\neq 0.

Extending

(\beta X_{v})(t+k)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=D\varphi^{k}(\beta(t)X_{v}(t))\hskip 22.99988pt\forall t\in I,k\in\mathbb{Z},

we have that
\beta X_{v}\in T_{\gamma}\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M
and thus we compute

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=d\mathscr{\mathscr{A}}^{(H,F)}_{\varphi}|_{(\gamma,\tau)}(\beta X_{v},0)

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{0}-\delta}^{t_{0}+\delta}d\lambda(\beta(t)X_{v}(t),\dot{\gamma}(t)-\tau X_{H_{t}}(\gamma(t))-X_{F_{t}}(\gamma(t)))dt

\displaystyle\neq 0.

Hence

\dot{\gamma}(t)=\tau X_{H_{t}}(\gamma(t))+X_{F_{t}}(\gamma(t))\hskip 22.99988pt\forall t\in I,

implying

\displaystyle\dot{\gamma}(t+k)

\displaystyle=D\varphi^{k}(\dot{\gamma}(t))

\displaystyle=\tau(D\varphi^{k}\circ X_{H_{t}})(\gamma(t))+(D\varphi^{k}\circ X_{F_{t}})(\gamma(t))

\displaystyle=\tau(D\varphi^{k}\circ X_{H_{t}}\circ\varphi^{-k}\circ\varphi^{k})(\gamma(t))+(D\varphi^{k}\circ X_{F_{t}}\circ\varphi^{-k}\circ\varphi^{k})(\gamma(t))

\displaystyle=\tau\varphi^{k}_{*}X_{H_{t}}(\gamma(t+k))+\varphi^{k}_{*}X_{F_{t}}(\gamma(t+k))

\displaystyle=\tau X_{\varphi^{k}_{*}H_{t}}(\gamma(t+k))+X_{\varphi^{k}_{*}F_{t}}(\gamma(t+k))

\displaystyle=\tau X_{H_{t+k}}(\gamma(t+k))+X_{F_{t+k}}(\gamma(t+k))

for all
t\in I
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. The other direction is immediate.
∎

Corollary 3.1.

The differential of the perturbed twisted Rabinowitz action functional is well-defined, that is, independent of the choice of twisted variation, and linear.

Preservation of energy
2.1
implies the following corollary.

Corollary 3.2.

Let
\varphi\in\Diff(M)
be an exact symplectomorphism of an exact symplectic manifold
(M,\lambda)
and
H\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi}(M)
. Then
\Crit\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}
consists precisely of all
(\gamma,\tau)\in\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\times\mathbb{R}
such that
\gamma(\mathbb{R})\subseteq H^{-1}(0)
and
\gamma
is an integral curve of
\tau X_{H}
.

There is a natural
\mathbb{R}
-action on the twisted loop space
\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M
given by

(s\cdot\gamma)(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\gamma(t+s)\hskip 22.99988pt\forall t\in\mathbb{R}.

If
(M,\lambda)
is an exact symplectic manifold and
H\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi}(M)
for an exact symplectomorphism
\varphi\in\Diff(M)
of finite order such that
\supp f\cap H^{-1}(0)=\varnothing
, then the twisted Rabinowitz action functional
\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}
is invariant under the induced
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-action on
\Crit\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}
. In particular, the unperturbed twisted Rabinowitz action functional is never a Morse function.

Definition 3.6 (Hessian of the Twisted Rabinowitz Action Functional).

Let
\varphi
be an exact symplectomorphism of an exact symplectic manifold
(M,\lambda)
and suppose that
H\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi}(M)
. For
(\gamma,\tau)\in\Crit\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}
, define the
Hessian of the twisted Rabinowitz action functional

\Hess\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}|_{(\gamma,\tau)}\colon(T_{\gamma}\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\times\mathbb{R})\times(T_{\gamma}\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\times\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}

by

\Hess\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}|_{(\gamma,\tau)}((X,\eta),(Y,\sigma))\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\varepsilon_{1}\partial\varepsilon_{2}}\bigg|_{\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{2}=0}\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}(\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}},\tau+\varepsilon_{1}\eta+\varepsilon_{2}\sigma),

for a smooth two-parameter family
\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}
of twisted loops with

\frac{\partial}{\partial\varepsilon_{1}}\bigg|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},0}=X\hskip 22.99988pt\text{and}\hskip 22.99988pt\frac{\partial}{\partial\varepsilon_{2}}\bigg|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{0,\varepsilon_{2}}=Y.

Remark 3.3.

Traditionally, the differential and the Hessian of the twisted Rabinowitz action functional are called the first and second variation of the twisted Rabinowitz action functional.

Definition 3.7 (Symplectic Connection).

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold. A
symplectic connection on
(M,\omega)
is defined to be a torsion-free connection
\nabla
in the tangent bundle
TM
such that
\nabla\omega=0
.

Remark 3.4.

Every symplectic manifold admits a symplectic connection by
[
Gut06
, p. 308]
, but in sharp contrast to the Riemannian case, a symplectic connection on a given symplectic manifold is in general not unique.

Lemma 3.2.

Let
\varphi\in\Diff(M)
be an exact symplectomorphism of an exact symplectic manifold
(M,\lambda)
. Fix a symplectic connection
\nabla
on
(M,d\lambda)
and a twisted Hamiltonian function
H\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi}(M)
. If
(\gamma,\tau)\in\Crit\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}
, then

\Hess\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}|_{(\gamma,\tau)}((X,\eta),(Y,\sigma))=\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda(Y,\nabla_{t}X)\\
-\tau\int_{0}^{1}\Hess^{\nabla}H(X,Y)-\eta\int_{0}^{1}dH(Y)-\sigma\int_{0}^{1}dH(X)

(14)

for all
(X,\eta),(Y,\sigma)\in T_{\gamma}\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\times\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

We compute

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\varepsilon_{1}\partial\varepsilon_{2}}\bigg|_{\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{2}=0}(\tau+\varepsilon_{1}\eta+\varepsilon_{2}\sigma)\int_{0}^{1}H\circ\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}\\
=\eta\int_{0}^{1}dH(Y)+\sigma\int_{0}^{1}dH(X)+\tau\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\varepsilon_{1}\partial\varepsilon_{2}}\bigg|_{\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{2}=0}H(\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}),

(15)

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\varepsilon_{1}\partial\varepsilon_{2}}\bigg|_{\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{2}=0}H(\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}})=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\varepsilon_{1}}\bigg|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}dH\mathinner{\bigl(\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}\bigr)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial}{\partial\varepsilon_{1}}\bigg|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}d\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(X_{H}(\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},0}),\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}\bigr)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-d\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(\nabla_{\varepsilon_{1}}|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}X_{H}(\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},0}),Y\bigr)}

\displaystyle-d\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(X_{H}(\gamma),\nabla_{\varepsilon_{1}}|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}\bigr)}.

The
d\lambda
-compatibility of
\nabla
implies

\displaystyle d\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(\nabla_{\varepsilon_{1}}|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}X_{H}(\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},0}),Y\bigr)}

\displaystyle=d\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(\nabla_{\partial_{\varepsilon_{1}}|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},0}}X_{H},Y\bigr)}

\displaystyle=d\lambda(\nabla_{X}X_{H},Y)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{X}d\lambda(X_{H},Y)-d\lambda(X_{H},\nabla_{X}Y)

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{X}dH(Y)+dH(\nabla_{X}Y)

\displaystyle=-X(Y(H))+(\nabla_{X}Y)H

\displaystyle=-\Hess^{\nabla}H|_{\gamma}(X,Y).

(16)

Next we compute

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\varepsilon_{1}\partial\varepsilon_{2}}\bigg|_{\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{2}=0}\int_{0}^{1}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}^{*}\lambda=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varepsilon_{1}}\bigg|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},0}^{*}i_{\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}}(d\lambda\circ\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},0})

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varepsilon_{1}}\bigg|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}d\gamma^{*}_{\varepsilon_{1},0}i_{\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}}(\lambda\circ\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varepsilon_{1}}\bigg|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}d\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}},\dot{\gamma}_{\varepsilon_{1},0}\bigr)}

\displaystyle+\frac{\partial}{\partial\varepsilon_{1}}\bigg|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}\lambda(\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}})|_{0}^{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(\nabla_{\varepsilon_{1}}|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}},\dot{\gamma}\bigr)}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(Y,\nabla_{\varepsilon_{1}}|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}\partial_{t}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},0}\bigr)}

\displaystyle+\frac{\partial}{\partial\varepsilon_{1}}\bigg|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}\lambda(\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}})|_{0}^{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(\nabla_{\varepsilon_{1}}|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}},X_{H}(\gamma)\bigr)}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(Y,\nabla_{t}\partial_{\varepsilon_{1}}|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},0}\bigr)}

\displaystyle+\frac{\partial}{\partial\varepsilon_{1}}\bigg|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}\lambda(\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}})|_{0}^{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\>\tau\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(\nabla_{\varepsilon_{1}}|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}},X_{H}(\gamma)\bigr)}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(Y,\nabla_{t}X\bigr)}

\displaystyle+\frac{\partial}{\partial\varepsilon_{1}}\bigg|_{\varepsilon_{1}=0}\lambda(\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}})|_{0}^{1}.

(17)

Moreover

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(Y,\nabla_{t}X\bigr)}=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{dt}d\lambda(Y,X)-\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda(\nabla_{t}Y,X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\>d\lambda(Y,X)|_{0}^{1}+\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda(X,\nabla_{t}Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\>d\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(D\varphi(Y(0)),D\varphi(X(0))\bigr)}-d\lambda(Y(0),X(0))

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda(X,\nabla_{t}Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda(X,\nabla_{t}Y).

(18)

Finally

\displaystyle\lambda(\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}})|_{0}^{1}

\displaystyle=\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(1)\bigr)}-\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(0)\bigr)}

\displaystyle=\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\varphi(\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(0))\bigr)}-\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(0)\bigr)}

\displaystyle=\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(D\varphi\mathinner{\bigl(\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(0)\bigr)}\bigr)}-\lambda\mathinner{\bigl(\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(0)\bigr)}

\displaystyle=(\varphi^{*}\lambda-\lambda)(\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(0))

\displaystyle=df_{\varphi}(\partial_{\varepsilon_{2}}|_{\varepsilon_{2}=0}\gamma_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}(0)).

(19)

Combining (
3.1
), (
15
), (
3.1
), and (
3.1
) yields (
14
).
∎

Corollary 3.3.

The Hessian of the twisted Rabinowitz action functional is a well-defined, that is, independent of the choice of twisted two-parameter family, symmetric bilinear form.

Lemma 3.3.

Let
\varphi\in\Diff(M)
be an exact symplectomorphism of an exact symplectic manifold
(M,\lambda)
and
H\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi}(M)
. If
(\gamma,\tau)\in\Crit\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}
, then

\Hess\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}|_{(\gamma,\tau)}((X,\eta),(Y,\sigma))=\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda(Y,L_{\tau X_{H}}X-\eta X_{H}(\gamma))\\
-\sigma\int_{0}^{1}dH(X)

(20)

for all
(X,\eta),(Y,\sigma)\in T_{\gamma}\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\times\mathbb{R}
, where

L_{\tau X_{H}}X(t)=\frac{d}{ds}\bigg|_{s=0}D\phi^{X_{H}}_{-s\tau}X(s+t)\hskip 22.99988pt\forall t\in\mathbb{R}.

Proof.

Inserting
\Hess^{\nabla}(X,Y)=d\lambda(Y,\nabla_{X}X_{H})
into (
14
) yields

\Hess\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}|_{(\gamma,\tau)}((X,\eta),(Y,\sigma))=\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda(Y,\nabla_{t}X-\tau\nabla_{X}X_{H})\\
-\eta\int_{0}^{1}dH(Y)-\sigma\int_{0}^{1}dH(X).

But as
\nabla
has no torsion by assumption, we compute

\nabla_{t}X-\tau\nabla_{X}X_{H}=\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}X-\tau\nabla_{X}X_{H}=\nabla_{\tau X_{H}}X-\tau\nabla_{X}X_{H}=[\tau X_{H},X],

and

\displaystyle[\tau X_{H},X](t)

\displaystyle=L_{\tau X_{H}}X(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{ds}\bigg|_{s=0}D\phi_{-s\tau}^{X_{H}}(X(\phi_{s\tau}^{X_{H}}(\gamma(t)))

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{ds}\bigg|_{s=0}D\phi_{-s\tau}^{X_{H}}(X(\phi_{s\tau}^{X_{H}}(\phi_{t\tau}^{X_{H}}(\gamma(0)))))

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{ds}\bigg|_{s=0}D\phi_{-s\tau}^{X_{H}}X(s+t)

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Corollary 3.4.

Let
\varphi\in\Diff(M)
be an exact symplectomorphism of an exact symplectic manifold
(M,\lambda)
and let
H\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi}(M)
. The kernel of the Hessian of the twisted Rabinowitz action functional
\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}
at
(\gamma,\tau)\in\Crit\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}
consists precisely of all
(X,\eta)\in T_{\gamma}\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\times\mathbb{R}
satisfying

L_{\tau X_{H}}X=\eta X_{H}(\gamma)\hskip 22.99988pt\text{and}\hskip 22.99988pt\int_{0}^{1}dH(X)=0.

Lemma 3.4.

Let
\varphi\in\Diff(M)
be an exact symplectomorphism of an exact symplectic manifold
(M,\lambda)
and
H\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi}(M)
. For every
(\gamma,\tau)\in\Crit\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}
, there is a canonical isomorphism

\ker\Hess\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}|_{(\gamma,\tau)}\cong\mathfrak{K}(\gamma,\tau),

(21)

where

\mathfrak{K}(\gamma,\tau)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\{(v_{0},\eta)\in T_{\gamma(0)}M\times\mathbb{R}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\textup{solution of \eqref{eq:initial_value_system}}\}}

with

D(\phi_{-\tau}^{X_{H}}\circ\varphi)v_{0}=v_{0}+\eta X_{H}(\gamma(0))\hskip 22.99988pt\text{and}\hskip 22.99988ptdH(v_{0})=0.

(22)

Proof.

We follow
[
FK18
, p. 99–100]
. Let
(X,\eta)\in\ker\Hess\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}|_{(\gamma,\tau)}
and define

v\colon I\to T_{\gamma(0)}M,\hskip 22.99988ptv(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=D\phi_{-\tau t}^{X_{H}}X(t).

We claim that

\ker\Hess\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}|_{(\gamma,\tau)}\to\mathfrak{K}(\gamma,\tau),\hskip 22.99988pt(X,\eta)\mapsto(v(0),\eta)

(23)

is an isomorphism. First, we show that the above homomorphism is indeed well-defined. The assumption that
(X,\eta)
lies in the kernel of the Hessian of the twisted Rabinowitz action functional at the critical point
(\gamma,\tau)
is by Corollary
3.4
equivalent to the system

\dot{v}=\eta X_{H}(\gamma(0))\hskip 22.99988pt\text{and}\hskip 22.99988pt\int_{0}^{1}dH(v)=0.

(24)

Integrating the first equation yields

v(t)=v_{0}+t\eta X_{H}(\gamma(0))\hskip 22.99988pt\forall t\in I,

with
v_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=v(0)
. Thus
(v_{0},\eta)\in\mathfrak{K}(\gamma,\tau)
follows from

\displaystyle v(1)

\displaystyle=D\phi_{-\tau}^{X_{H}}X(1)

\displaystyle=D\phi_{-\tau}^{X_{H}}D\varphi(X(0))

\displaystyle=D(\phi_{-\tau}^{X_{H}}\circ\varphi)X(0)

\displaystyle=D(\phi_{-\tau}^{X_{H}}\circ\varphi)v_{0}.

(25)

That (
23
) is an isomorphism follows by considering the inverse

\mathfrak{K}(\gamma,\tau)\to\ker\Hess\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}|_{(\gamma,\tau)},\hskip 22.99988pt(v_{0},\eta)\mapsto(X,\eta),

where
X\in T_{\gamma}\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M
is defined by

X(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=D\phi^{X_{H}}_{\tau t}(v_{0}+t\eta X_{H}(\gamma(0)))\hskip 22.99988pt\forall t\in\mathbb{R}.

This establishes the canonical isomorphism (
23
).
∎

Recall, that a strict contactomorphism of a contact manifold
(\Sigma,\alpha)
is defined to be a diffeomorphism
\varphi\in\Diff(\Sigma)
such that
\varphi^{*}\alpha=\alpha
.

Definition 3.8 (Parametrised Twisted Reeb Orbit).

For a contact manifold
(\Sigma,\alpha)
and a strict contactomorphism
\varphi\colon(\Sigma,\alpha)\to(\Sigma,\alpha)
define the set of
parametrised twisted Reeb orbits on
(\Sigma,\alpha)
by

\mathscr{P}_{\varphi}(\Sigma,\alpha)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\{(\gamma,\tau)\in\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\dot{\gamma}(t)=\tau R(\gamma(t))\>\forall t\in\mathbb{R}\}}.

Definition 3.9 (Twisted Spectrum).

For a contact manifold
(\Sigma,\alpha)
and a strict contactomorphism
\varphi\colon(\Sigma,\alpha)\to(\Sigma,\alpha)
define the
twisted spectrum
by

\Spec(\Sigma,\alpha)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\{\tau\in\mathbb{R}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\exists\gamma\in\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}\Sigma\>\text{such that }(\gamma,\tau)\in\mathscr{P}_{\varphi}(\Sigma,\alpha)\}}.

Lemma 3.5.

Let
\varphi\colon(\Sigma,\alpha)\to(\Sigma,\alpha)
be a strict contactomorphism of a compact contact manifold
(\Sigma,\alpha)
. Then

\varphi\circ\phi^{R}_{t}=\phi^{R}_{t}\circ\varphi\hskip 22.99988pt\forall t\in\mathbb{R}.

Proof.

If
\varphi_{*}R=R
