\displaystyle=\Omega(v^{-})-\mathscr{A}_{r}(u_{k}(s_{0}),\tau_{k}(s_{0}),s_{0})+\mathinner{\lVert F\rVert}

for all
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Similarly, we compute

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\infty}^{s_{0}}d\mathscr{A}_{r}(\partial_{s}(u_{k},\tau_{k}),s)ds

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{s_{0}}\frac{d}{ds}\mathscr{A}_{r}(u_{k},\tau_{k},s)ds-\int_{-\infty}^{s_{0}}(\partial_{s}\mathscr{A}_{r})(u_{k},\tau_{k},s)ds

\displaystyle=\mathscr{A}_{r}(u_{k}(s_{0}),\tau_{k}(s_{0}),s_{0})-\lim_{s\to-\infty}\mathscr{A}_{r}(u_{k},\tau_{k},s)-\int_{-\infty}^{s_{0}}(\partial_{s}\mathscr{A}_{r})(u_{k},\tau_{k},s)ds

\displaystyle=\mathscr{A}_{r}(u_{k}(s_{0}),\tau_{k}(s_{0}),s_{0})-\mathscr{A}_{0}(v^{-},\tau^{-})+\int_{-\infty}^{s_{0}}\dot{\beta}_{r}(s)\int_{0}^{1}F_{t}(u_{k}(s,t))dtds

and thus we estimate

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{r}(u_{k}(s_{0}),\tau_{k}(s_{0}),s_{0})

\displaystyle\geq\mathscr{A}_{0}(v^{-},\tau^{-})-\int_{-\infty}^{s_{0}}\dot{\beta}_{r}(s)\int_{0}^{1}F_{t}(u_{k}(s,t))dtds

\displaystyle\geq\mathscr{A}_{0}(v^{-},\tau^{-})-\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathinner{\lVert\dot{\beta}(s)F\rVert}_{+}ds

\displaystyle\geq\Omega(v^{-})-\mathinner{\lVert F\rVert}.

This completes the proof of the Forcing Theorem
4.3
.
∎

We conlcude this section by applying the Forcing Theorem
4.3
to a displaceable twisted stable hypersurface.

Example 4.5.

Consider the isometry

\varphi\colon\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2},\hskip 22.99988pt\varphi(q_{1},q_{2})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(q_{2},-q_{1})

and its cotangent lift

D\varphi^{\dagger}\colon\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2},\hskip 11.49994ptD\varphi^{\dagger}(q_{1},q_{2},p_{1},p_{2})=(q_{2},-q_{1},p_{2},-p_{1}).

Then
\Sigma_{c}\subseteq(T^{*}\mathbb{T}^{2},\omega_{\sigma},H)
is a displaceable twisted stable hypersurface for the area form
\sigma=dq_{1}\wedge dq_{2}
by Example
4.1
. By Example
4.2
, we have that

v\colon\mathbb{R}\to\Sigma_{c},\hskip 22.99988ptv(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sqrt{2c}(\sin t,\cos t,\cos t,-\sin t)

is a
\tau
-periodic twisted characteristic for all periods
\tau\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}+\frac{\pi}{2}
and
c>0
. Thus if we choose
v^{-}\in\mathscr{C}_{\varphi}(\Sigma_{c})
of period
\tau>0
, then we compute for
v\in\mathscr{C}_{\varphi}(\Sigma_{c})
of period
\tau+2\pi

\Omega(v)-\Omega(v^{-})=c(\tau+2\pi)-c\tau=2\pi c=e(\Sigma_{c})

by Example
4.2
and Example
2.16
. Hence, we have verified the statement of the Forcing Theorem
4.3
for the displaceable twisted stable hypersurface
\Sigma_{c}
in the symplectically aspherical and geometrically bounded symplectic manifold
(T^{*}\mathbb{T}^{2},\omega_{\sigma})
. Indeed,
(T^{*}\mathbb{T}^{2},\omega_{\sigma})
is geometrically bounded by Example
4.4
, and symplectically aspherical as

\pi_{2}(T^{*}\mathbb{T}^{n})\cong\pi_{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n})\times\pi_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cong 0.

5
Further Steps in Twisted Rabinowitz–Floer Homology

In this final chapter we discuss some possible further research in twisted Rabinowitz–Floer homology for future work. One can of course try to find a twisted version of every result provided by standard Rabinowitz–Floer homology. Following the survey article
[
AF12a
]
, major results relate Rabinowitz–Floer homology to symplectic homology.

In the first section we briefly outline a further computation of twisted Rabinowitz–Floer homology, where the hypersurface is not displaceable.

In the second section, we explain an important physical setting where the Forcing Theorem
4.3
and Theorem
1.1
might be applicable.

5.1
Cotangent Bundles

Let
(M,g)
be a compact connected Riemannian manifold and let
(S^{*}M,pdq)
be the spherisation of
M
as in Example
2.11
. By
[
AS09
]
or
[
CFO10
, Theorem 1.10]
, we have

\RFH_{k}(S^{*}M,T^{*}M)\cong\begin{cases}\operatorname{H}^{-k+1}(\mathscr{L}M)&k<0,\\
\operatorname{H}_{k}(\mathscr{L}M)&k>1.\end{cases}

In the degrees
k=0,1
the answer is known and depends on the Euler class. The proof uses a relation between Rabinowitz–Floer homology and symplectic homology, respectively symplectic cohomology. If
\varphi\in\Aut(D^{*}M,pdq|_{D^{*}M})
is a Liouville automorphism, then it is plausible to expect

\RFH_{k}^{\varphi}(S^{*}M,T^{*}M)\cong\begin{cases}\operatorname{H}^{-k+1}(\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M)&k<0,\\
\operatorname{H}_{k}(\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M)&k>1.\end{cases}

However, it would be also interesting to study the loop space homology
\operatorname{H}_{\ast}(\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M)
itself, because usually one computes the free loop space homology via Morse theory. For details see
[
LO15
, Chapter 2]
.

5.2
Stark–Zeeman Systems

Following
[
CFZ19
]
we introduce Stark–Zeeman systems. For
\mu_{\pm}>0
define the potential functions

V_{\pm}\colon\mathbb{C}\setminus\{\pm 1\}\to\mathbb{R},\hskip 22.99988ptV_{\pm}(z)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=-\frac{\mu_{\pm}}{\mathinner{\lvert z\pm 1\rvert}}.

Let
U_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
be open and star-shaped with respect to the origin such that
\pm 1\in U_{0}
. Choose
V_{0}\in C^{\infty}(U_{0})
. Moreover, set

V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=V_{+}+V_{-}+V_{0}\in C^{\infty}(U)

for
U\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=U_{0}\setminus\{\pm 1\}
. For a function
B\in C^{\infty}(U_{0})
, let
\sigma_{B}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=Bdq_{1}\wedge dq_{2}
and abbreviate by
(T^{*}U,\omega_{B})
the associated magnetic cotangent bundle. Fix a Riemannian metric
g
on
U_{0}
which is conformal to the standard metric. A
Stark–Zeeman system
is the magnetic Hamiltonian system
(T^{*}U,\omega_{B},H)
with

H(q,p)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\lVert p\rVert}^{2}_{g^{*}}+V(q)\hskip 22.99988pt\forall(q,p)\in T^{*}U.

For
c\in\mathbb{R}
a regular value of
H
, we consider a connected component
\Sigma_{c}\subseteq H^{-1}(c)
such that
\mathfrak{K}_{c}\cup\{\pm 1\}
is bounded and simply connected, where the Hill’s region
\mathfrak{K}_{c}
is defined by

\mathfrak{K}_{c}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\pi(\Sigma_{c})\subseteq\{q\in U\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V(q)\leq c\}.

For example, the planar circular restricted three-body problem is a Stark–Zeeman system. In order to deal with collisions, we regularise
\Sigma_{c}
.

Definition 5.1 (Regularisation,
[
FK18
, p. 48]
).

Let
(\Sigma,\omega)
be a noncompact Hamiltonian manifold. A
regularisation of
(\Sigma,\omega)
is defined to be a compact Hamiltonian manifold
(\overline{\Sigma},\overline{\omega})
such that there exists an embedding
\iota\colon\Sigma\hookrightarrow\overline{\Sigma}
with
\iota^{*}\overline{\omega}=\omega
.

Consider the
Birkhoff regularisation map

\varphi\colon\mathbb{C}^{*}\to\mathbb{C},\hskip 22.99988ptB(z)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\biggl(z+\frac{1}{z}\biggr)}.

By Example
2.4
, the cotangent lift
D\varphi^{\dagger}
of
\varphi
is given by

D\varphi^{\dagger}\colon T^{*}\mathbb{C}^{*}\to T^{*}\mathbb{C},\hskip 22.99988ptD\varphi^{\dagger}(z,w)=\mathinner{\biggl(\frac{z^{2}+1}{2z},\frac{2\overline{z}^{2}w}{\overline{z}^{2}-1}\biggr)}=(q,p).

The regular energy surface
\Sigma_{c}
gives rise to a regular energy surface
\Sigma^{B}_{c}\subseteq K^{-1}(0)
, where the rescaled Hamiltonian
K\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=H\circ D\varphi^{\dagger}
is given by

K(z,w)=\frac{\mathinner{\lVert w\rVert}^{2}_{g^{*}}}{2}-\frac{\mu_{+}\mathinner{\lvert z+1\rvert}^{2}}{2\mathinner{\lvert z\rvert}^{3}}-\frac{\mu_{-}\mathinner{\lvert z-1\rvert}^{2}}{2\mathinner{\lvert z\rvert}^{3}}+\frac{(V_{0}(q)-c)\mathinner{\lvert z^{2}-1\rvert}^{2}}{4\mathinner{\lvert z\rvert}^{4}}.

The compact regular energy surface
\Sigma^{B}_{c}
is called the
Birkhoff regularisaton of
\Sigma_{c}
. This regularisation is invariant under the cotangent lift

\Phi\colon T^{*}\mathbb{C}^{*}\to T^{*}\mathbb{C}^{*},\hskip 22.99988pt\Phi(z,w)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\biggl(\frac{1}{z},-\overline{z}^{2}w\biggr)}

As the induced action of
\Phi
on
\Sigma_{c}^{B}
is free, we obtain the cover

\Sigma^{B}_{c}\to\Sigma^{B}_{c}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

Explicitly, there exist diffeomorphisms such that

\Sigma^{B}_{c}\cong\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\hskip 22.99988pt\text{and}\hskip 22.99988pt\Sigma^{B}_{c}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong\mathbb{RP}^{3}\#\mathbb{RP}^{3}.

Therefore, it may be possible to apply the ideas developed in the proof of Theorem
1.1
or the Forcing Theorem
4.3
to these hypersurfaces. The analysis of these hypersurfaces is already quite delicate in the special case of the planar circular restricted three-body problem. Indeed, it requires some work to show that the regularised energy hypersurface is fibrewise star-shaped for energy values below the first critical value. For details, see
[
FK18
, Theorem 5.7.2]
. Hence we cannot expect stability of the hypersurfaces in a general Stark–Zeeman system.

Appendix A
Twisted Loops in Universal Covering Manifolds

In this Appendix, we will consider the category of topological manifolds rather than the category of smooth manifolds, because smoothness does not add much to the discussion. Free and based loop spaces are fundamental objects in Algebraic Topology, for a vast treatment of the geometry and topology of based as well as free loop spaces see for example
[
LO15
]
. But so-called twisted loop spaces are not considered that much.

Theorem A.1 (Twisted Loops in Universal Covering Manifolds).

Let
(M,x)
be a connected pointed topological manifold and
\pi\colon\tilde{M}\to M
the universal covering.

(a)

Fix
[\eta]\in\pi_{1}(M,x)
and denote by
U_{\eta}\subseteq\mathscr{L}(M,x)
the path component corresponding to
[\eta]
via the bijection
\pi_{0}(\mathscr{L}(M,x))\cong\pi_{1}(M,x)
. For every
e,e^{\prime}\in\pi^{-1}(x)
and
\varphi\in\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})
such that
\varphi(e)=\tilde{\eta}_{e}(1)
, where
\tilde{\eta}_{e}
denotes the unique lift of
\eta
with
\tilde{\eta}_{e}(0)=e
, we have a commutative diagram of homeomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}(\tilde{M},e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}_{\psi\circ\varphi\circ\psi^{-1}}(\tilde{M},e^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\eta},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{e^{\prime}}}

(38)

where
\psi\in\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})
is such that
\psi(e)=e^{\prime}
,

\hskip 22.99988ptL_{\psi}\colon\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}(\tilde{M},e)\to\mathscr{L}_{\psi\circ\varphi\circ\psi^{-1}}(\tilde{M},e^{\prime}),\hskip 22.99988ptL_{\psi}(\gamma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\psi\circ\gamma,

and

\displaystyle\Psi_{e}\colon U_{\eta}\to\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}(\tilde{M},e),

\displaystyle\Psi_{e}(\gamma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\tilde{\gamma}_{e},

\displaystyle\Psi_{e^{\prime}}\colon U_{\eta}\to\mathscr{L}_{\psi\circ\varphi\circ\psi^{-1}}(\tilde{M},e^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\Psi_{e^{\prime}}(\gamma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\tilde{\gamma}_{e^{\prime}}.

Moreover,
U_{c_{x}}\cong\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}(\tilde{M},e)
via
\Psi_{e}
if and only if
\varphi=\id_{\tilde{M}}
, where
c_{x}
denotes the constant loop at
x
.

(b)

For every
\varphi\in\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})
and
e,e^{\prime}\in\pi^{-1}(x)
we have a commutative diagram of isomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(M,x),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{e^{\prime}}}

where for
\psi\in\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})
sucht that
\psi(e)=e^{\prime}

\hskip 22.99988ptC_{\psi}\colon\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})\to\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M}),\hskip 22.99988ptC_{\psi}(\varphi)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\psi\circ\varphi\circ\psi^{-1},

and

\displaystyle\Phi_{e}\colon\pi_{1}(M,x)\to\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M}),

\displaystyle\Phi_{e}([\gamma])\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\varphi^{e}_{[\gamma]},

\displaystyle\Phi_{e^{\prime}}\colon\pi_{1}(M,x)\to\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M}),

\displaystyle\Phi_{e^{\prime}}([\gamma])\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\varphi^{e^{\prime}}_{[\gamma]},

with
\varphi^{e}_{[\gamma]}(e)=\tilde{\gamma}_{e}(1)
and
\varphi^{e^{\prime}}_{[\gamma]}(e^{\prime})=\tilde{\gamma}_{e^{\prime}}(1)
.

(c)

The projection

\hskip 22.99988pt\tilde{\pi}_{x}\colon\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi\in\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})\\
e\in\pi^{-1}(x)\end{subarray}}\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}(\tilde{M},e)\to\mathscr{L}(M,x)

defined by
\tilde{\pi}_{x}(\gamma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\pi\circ\gamma
is a covering map with number of sheets coinciding with the cardinality of
\pi_{1}(M,x)
. Moreover,
\tilde{\pi}_{x}
restricts to define a covering map

\hskip 22.99988pt\tilde{\pi}_{x}|_{\id_{\tilde{M}}}\colon\coprod_{e\in\pi^{-1}(x)}\mathscr{L}(\tilde{M},e)\to U_{c_{x}},

and
\tilde{\pi}_{x}
gives rise to a principal
\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})
-bundle. If
M
admits a smooth structure, then this bundle is additionally a bundle of smooth Banach manifolds.

Proof.

For proving part (a), fix a path class
[\gamma]\in\pi_{1}(M,x)
. As any topological manifold is Hausdorff, paracompact and locally metrisable by definition, the Smirnov Metrisation Theorem
[
Mun00
, Theorem 42.1]
implies that
M
is metrisable. Let
d
be a metric on
M
and
\bar{d}
be the standard bounded metric corresponding to
d
, that is,

\bar{d}(x,y)=\min\mathinner{\{d(x,y),1\}}\hskip 22.99988pt\forall x,y\in M.

The metric
\bar{d}
induces the same topology on
M
as
d
by
[
Mun00
, Theorem 20.1]
. Topologise the based loop space
\mathscr{L}(M,x)\subseteq\mathscr{L}M
as a subspace of the free loop space on
M
, where
\mathscr{L}M
is equipped with the topology of uniform convergence, that is, with the supremum metric

\bar{d}_{\infty}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{S}^{1}}\bar{d}\mathinner{\bigl(\gamma(t),\gamma^{\prime}(t)\bigr)}\hskip 22.99988pt\forall\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathscr{L}M.

There is a canonical pseudometric on the universal covering manifold
\tilde{M}
induced by
\bar{d}
given by
\bar{d}\circ\pi
. As every pseudometric generates a topology, we topologise the based twisted loop space
\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}(\tilde{M},e)\subseteq\mathscr{P}\tilde{M}
as a subspace of the free path space on
\tilde{M}
for every
e\in\pi^{-1}(x)
via the supremum metric
\tilde{d}_{\infty}
corresponding to
\bar{d}\circ\pi
. In fact,
\tilde{d}_{\infty}
is a metric as if
\tilde{d}_{\infty}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})=0
, then by definition of
\tilde{d}_{\infty}
we have that
\pi(\gamma)=\pi(\gamma^{\prime})
. But as
\gamma(0)=e=\gamma^{\prime}(0)
, we conclude
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}
by the unique lifting property of paths
[
Lee11
, Corollary 11.14]
. Note that the resulting topology of uniform convergence on
\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}(\tilde{M},e)
coincides with the compact-open topology by
[
Mun00
, Theorem 46.8]
or
[
Hat01
, Proposition A.13]
. In particular, the topology of uniform convergence does not depend on the choice of a metric (see
[
Mun00
, Corollary 46.9]
). It follows from
[
Lee11
, Theorem 11.15 (b)]
, that
\Psi_{e}
and
\Psi_{e^{\prime}}
are well-defined. Moreover, it is immediate by the fact that the projection
\pi\colon\tilde{M}\to M
is an isometry with respect to the above metric, that
\Psi_{e}
and
\Psi_{e^{\prime}}
are continuous with continuous inverse given by the composition with
\pi
. It is also immediate that
L_{\psi}
is continuous with continuous inverse
L_{\psi^{-1}}
.

Next we show that the diagram (
38
) commutes. Note that

\pi\circ L_{\psi}\circ\Psi_{e}=\pi\circ\Psi_{e}=\id_{U_{\eta}}=\pi\circ\Psi_{e^{\prime}},

thus by

(L_{\psi}\circ\Psi_{e}(\gamma))(0)=\psi(\tilde{\gamma}_{e}(0))=\psi(e)=e^{\prime}=\tilde{\gamma}_{e^{\prime}}(0)=\Psi_{e^{\prime}}(\gamma)(0)

and by uniqueness it follows that

L_{\psi}\circ\Psi_{e}=\Psi_{e^{\prime}}.

In particular

\Psi_{e^{\prime}}(1)=(L_{\psi}\circ\Psi_{e})(1)=\psi(\varphi(e))=(\psi\circ\varphi\circ\psi^{-1})(e^{\prime}),

and thus
\Psi_{e^{\prime}}(\gamma)\in\mathscr{L}_{\psi\circ\varphi\circ\psi^{-1}}(\tilde{M},e^{\prime})
. Consequently, the homeomorphism
\Psi_{e^{\prime}}
is well-defined.

Recall, that by the Monodromy Theorem
[
Lee11
, Theorem 11.15 (b)]

\gamma\simeq\gamma^{\prime}\hskip 22.99988pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 22.99988pt\Psi_{e}(\gamma)(1)=\Psi_{e}(\gamma^{\prime})(1)

for all paths
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
in
M
starting at
x
and ending at the same point. Note that the statement of the the Monodromy Theorem is an if-and-only-if statement since
\tilde{M}
is simply connected.

Suppose
\gamma\in\mathscr{L}(M,x)
is contractible. Then
\gamma\simeq c_{x}
, implying
e\in\Fix(\varphi)
. But the only deck transformation of
\pi
fixing any point of
\tilde{M}
is
\id_{\tilde{M}}
by
[
Lee11
, Proposition 12.1 (a)]
.

Conversely, assume that
\gamma\in\mathscr{L}(M,x)
is not contractible. Then we have that
\Psi_{e}(\gamma)(1)\neq e
. Indeed, if
\Psi_{e}(\gamma)(1)=e
, then
\gamma\simeq c_{x}
and consequently,
\gamma
would be contractible. As normal covering maps have transitive automorphism groups by
[
Lee11
, Corollary 12.5]
, there exists
\psi\in\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})\setminus\mathinner{\{\id_{\tilde{M}}\}}
such that
\Psi_{e}(\gamma)(1)=\psi(e)
.

For proving part (b), observe that
\Phi_{e}
and
\Phi_{e^{\prime}}
are isomorphisms follows from
[
Lee11
, Corollary 12.9]
. Moreover, it is also clear that
C_{\psi}
is an isomorphism with inverse
C_{\psi^{-1}}
. Let
[\gamma]\in\pi_{1}(M,x)
. Then using part (a) we compute

\displaystyle(C_{\psi}\circ\Phi_{e})[\gamma](e^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\psi\circ\Phi_{e}[\gamma]\circ\psi^{-1})(e^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\psi\mathinner{\bigl(\varphi_{[\gamma]}^{e}(e)\bigr)}

\displaystyle=\psi(\tilde{\gamma}_{e}(1))

\displaystyle=(L_{\psi}\circ\Psi_{e})(\gamma)(1)

\displaystyle=\Psi_{e^{\prime}}(\gamma)(1)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\gamma}_{e^{\prime}}(1)

\displaystyle=\varphi^{e^{\prime}}_{[\gamma]}(e^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\Phi_{e^{\prime}}[\gamma](e^{\prime}).

Thus by uniqueness
[
Lee11
, Proposition 12.1 (a)]
, we conclude

C_{\psi}\circ\Phi_{e}=\Phi_{e^{\prime}}.

Finally for proving (c), define a metric
\tilde{d}_{\infty}
on

E\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi\in\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})\\
e\in\pi^{-1}(x)\end{subarray}}\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}(\tilde{M},e)

by

\tilde{d}_{\infty}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\begin{cases}\bar{d}_{\infty}\mathinner{\bigl(\pi(\gamma),\pi(\gamma^{\prime})\bigr)}&\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}(\tilde{M},e),\\
1&\text{else}.\end{cases}

Then the induced topology coincides with the disjoint union topology and with respect to this topology,
\tilde{\pi}_{x}
is continuous. So left to show is that
\tilde{\pi}_{x}
is a covering map. Surjectivity is clear. So let
\gamma\in\mathscr{L}(M,x)
. Then
\gamma\in U_{\eta}
for some
[\eta]\in\pi_{1}(M,x)
. Now note that
U_{\eta}
is open in
\mathscr{L}(M,x)
and by part (a) we conclude

\tilde{\pi}_{x}^{-1}(U_{\eta})=\coprod_{\psi\in\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})}\mathscr{L}_{\psi\circ\varphi\circ\psi^{-1}}(\tilde{M},\psi(e))

(39)

for some fixed
e\in\pi^{-1}(x)
and
\varphi\in\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})
such that
\varphi(e)=\tilde{\eta}_{e}(1)
.

As the cardinality of the fibre
\pi^{-1}(x)
and of
\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})
coincides with the cardinality of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(M,x)
by
[
Lee11
, Corollary 11.31]
and part (b), we conclude that the number of sheets is equal to the cardinality of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(M,x)
.

Equip
\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})
with the discrete topology. As the fundamental group of every topological manifold is countable by
[
Lee11
, Theorem 7.21]
, we have that
\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})
is a discrete topological Lie group. Now label the distinct path classes in
\pi_{1}(M,x)
by
\beta\in B
and for fixed
e\in\pi^{-1}(x)
define local trivialisations

(\tilde{\pi}_{x},\alpha_{\beta})\colon\tilde{\pi}_{x}^{-1}(U_{\beta})\xrightarrow{\cong}U_{\beta}\times\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M}),

making use of (
39
) by

\alpha_{\beta}(\gamma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\psi^{-1},

whenever
\gamma\in\mathscr{L}_{\psi\circ\varphi\circ\psi^{-1}}(\tilde{M},\psi(e))
. Consequently,
\tilde{\pi}_{x}
is a fibre bundle with discrete fibre
\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})
and bundle atlas
(U_{\beta},\alpha_{\beta})_{\beta\in B}
. Define a free right action

E\times\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})\to E,\hskip 22.99988pt\gamma\cdot\xi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\xi^{-1}\circ\gamma.

Then
\alpha_{\beta}
is
\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})
-equivariant with respect to this action for all
\beta\in B
. Indeed, using again the commutative diagram (
38
) we compute

\alpha_{\beta}(\gamma\cdot\xi)=\alpha_{\beta}(\xi^{-1}\circ\gamma)=\mathinner{\bigl(\xi^{-1}\circ\psi\bigr)}^{-1}=\psi^{-1}\circ\xi=\alpha_{\beta}(\gamma)\circ\xi

for all
\xi\in\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})
and
\gamma\in\mathscr{L}_{\psi\circ\varphi\circ\psi^{-1}}(\tilde{M},\psi(e))
. Note, that here we use again the fact that
\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})
acts transitively on the fibre
\pi^{-1}(x)
.

Suppose that
M
admits a smooth structure. Then for every compact smooth manifold
N
we have that the mapping space
C(N,M)
admits the structure of a smooth Banach manifold by
[
Wit19
]
. By
[
LO15
, Theorem 1.1 p. 24]
, there is a smooth fibre bundle, called the
loop-loop fibre bundle
,

\mathscr{L}(M,x)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{L}M\xrightarrow{\ev_{0}}M

where

\ev_{0}\colon\mathscr{L}M\to M,\hskip 22.99988pt\ev_{0}(\gamma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\gamma(0).

Thus the based loop space
\mathscr{L}(M,x)=\ev_{0}^{-1}(x)
on
M
is a smooth Banach manifold by the implicit function theorem
[
MS12
, Theorem A.3.3]
for all
x\in M
. Likewise, by
[
LO15
, Theorem 1.2 p. 25]
, there is a smooth fibre bundle, called the
path-loop fibre bundle
,

\mathscr{L}(\tilde{M},e)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{P}(\tilde{M},e)\xrightarrow{\ev_{1}}\tilde{M},

where

\mathscr{P}(\tilde{M},e)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{\gamma\in C(I,\tilde{M})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\gamma(0)=e\}

denotes the based path space and

\ev_{1}\colon\mathscr{P}(\tilde{M},e)\to\tilde{M},\hskip 22.99988pt\ev_{1}(\gamma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\gamma(1).

Therefore, the twisted loop space
\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}(\tilde{M},e)=\ev_{1}^{-1}(\varphi(e))
is also a smooth Banach manifold for all
\varphi\in\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})
and
e\in\pi^{-1}(x)
by the implicit function theorem
[
MS12
, Theorem A.3.3]
. As the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(M,x)
is countable, the topological space
E
has only countably many connected components being smooth Banach manifolds and thus the total space itself is a smooth Banach manifold. Finally,
\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})
is trivially a Banach manifold with
\dim\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})=0
as a discrete Lie group.
∎

Corollary A.1.

Let
(M,x)
be a connected pointed topological manifold and denote by
\pi\colon\tilde{M}\to M
the universal covering of
M
. Assume that
\pi_{1}(M,x)
is abelian.

(a)

Fix a path class
[\eta]\in\pi_{1}(M,x)
. For every
e,e^{\prime}\in\pi^{-1}(x)
and deck transformation
\varphi\in\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})
such that
\varphi(e)=\tilde{\eta}_{e}(1)
, we have a commutative diagram of homeomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}(\tilde{M},e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}(\tilde{M},e^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\eta},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{e^{\prime}}}

where
\psi\in\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})
is such that
\psi(e)=e^{\prime}
.

(b)

For every
\varphi\in\Aut_{\pi}(\tilde{M})
we have that
\Phi_{e}=\Phi_{e^{\prime}}
for all
e,e^{\prime}\in\pi^{-1}(x)
.

Lemma
1.1
now follows from part (a) of Theorem
A.1
. Indeed, by assumption
\varphi\in\Aut_{\pi}(\Sigma)\setminus\{\id_{\Sigma}\}
and using the long exact sequence of homotopy groups of a fibration
[
Hat01
, Theorem 4.41]
, there is a short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(\Sigma,x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(\Sigma/\mathbb{Z}_{m},\pi(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

In particular, by
[
Lee11
, Corollary 12.9]
we conclude

\Aut_{\pi}(\Sigma)\cong\pi_{1}(\Sigma/\mathbb{Z}_{m},\pi(x))\cong\mathbb{Z}_{m}\cong\{\id_{\Sigma},\varphi,\dots,\varphi^{m-1}\}.

Finally, we discuss a smooth structure on the continuous free twisted loop space of a smooth manifold.

Lemma A.1.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold and
\varphi\in\Diff(M)
. Then the continuous free twisted loop space
\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M
is the pullback of

(\ev_{0},\ev_{1})\colon\mathscr{P}M\to M\times M,\hskip 22.99988pt\gamma\mapsto(\gamma(0),\gamma(1)),

where we abbreviate
\mathscr{P}M\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=C(I,M)
, along the graph of
\varphi

\Gamma_{\varphi}\colon M\to M\times M,\hskip 22.99988pt\Gamma_{\varphi}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(x,\varphi(x)),

in the category of smooth Banach manifolds. Moreover, we have that

T_{\gamma}\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M=\{X\in\Gamma^{0}(\gamma^{*}TM)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X(1)=D\varphi(X(0))\}

for all
\gamma\in\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M
.

Proof.

Write
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\ev_{0},\ev_{1})
. Then

\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M=f^{-1}(\Gamma_{\varphi}(M)).

Thus in order to show that the free twisted loop space
\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M
is a smooth Banach manifold, it is enough to show that
f
is transverse to the properly embedded smooth submanifold
\Gamma_{\varphi}(M)\subseteq M\times M
. By
[
Lan99
, Proposition 2.4]
we need to show that the composition

\Phi_{\gamma}\colon T_{\gamma}\mathscr{P}M\xrightarrow{Df_{\gamma}}T_{(x,\varphi(x))}(M\times M)\to T_{(x,\varphi(x))}(M\times M)/T_{(x,\varphi(x))}\Gamma_{\varphi}(M)

is surjective and
\ker\Phi_{\gamma}
is complemented for all
\gamma\in f^{-1}(\Gamma_{\varphi}(M))
, where we abbreviate
x\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\gamma(0)
. Note that we have a canonical isomorphism

T_{(x,\varphi(x))}(M\times M)/T_{(x,\varphi(x))}\Gamma_{\varphi}(M)\to T_{\varphi(x)}M,\hskip 11.49994pt[(v,u)]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=u-D\varphi(v).

Under this canonical isomorphism, the linear map
\Phi_{\gamma}
is given by

\Phi_{\gamma}(X)=X(1)-D\varphi(X(0)),\hskip 22.99988pt\forall X\in\Gamma^{0}(\gamma^{*}TM).

Fix a Riemannian metric on
M
and let
X_{v}\in\Gamma(\gamma^{*}TM)
be the unique parallel vector field with
X_{v}(1)=v\in T_{\varphi(x)}M
. Fix a cutoff function
\beta\in C^{\infty}(I)
such that
\supp\beta\subseteq\mathinner{\bigl[\frac{1}{2},1\bigr]}
and
\beta=1
in a neighbourhood of
1
. Then
\Phi_{\gamma}(\beta X_{v})=v
and consequently,
\Phi_{\gamma}
is surjective. Moreover

\ker\Phi_{\gamma}=\mathinner{\{X\in\Gamma^{0}(\gamma^{*}TM)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X(1)=D\varphi(X(0))\}}

is complemented by the finite-dimensional vector space

V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\{\beta X_{v}\in\Gamma(\gamma^{*}TM)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}v\in T_{\varphi(x)}M\}}.

Indeed, any
X\in\Gamma^{0}(\gamma^{*}TM)
can be decomposed uniquely as

X=X-\beta X_{v}+\beta X_{v},\hskip 22.99988ptv\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=X(1)-D\varphi(X(0)).

Abbreviating
Y\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=X-\beta X_{v}\in\Gamma^{0}(\gamma^{*}TM)
, we have that

Y(1)=D\varphi(X(0))=D\varphi(Y(0)),

implying
Y\in\ker\Phi_{\gamma}
. Thus
\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M
is a smooth Banach manifold.

Now note that
\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M
can be identified with the pullback

f^{*}\mathscr{P}M=\{(x,\gamma)\in M\times\mathscr{P}M\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(\gamma(0),\gamma(1))=\mathinner{(x,\varphi(x))}\},

making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\mathscr{P}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\times M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{\varphi}}

commute, via the homeomorphism

\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\to f^{*}\mathscr{P}M,\hskip 22.99988pt\gamma\mapsto(\gamma(0),\gamma).

Finally, one computes

T_{(x,\gamma)}f^{*}\mathscr{P}M=\{(v,X)\in T_{x}M\times T_{\gamma}\mathscr{P}M\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Df_{\gamma}X=D\Gamma_{\varphi}|_{x}(v)\}

for all
(x,\gamma)\in f^{*}\mathscr{P}M
.
∎

Remark A.1.

Using Lemma
A.1
one should be able to prove similar results as in Theorem
A.1
in the case of free twisted loop spaces. However, in the non-abelian case the situation gets much more complicated as in general it is not true, that lifts of conjugated elements of the fundamental group lie in the same free twisted loop space by
[
LO15
, Theorem 1.6 (i)]
.

Appendix B
On the Nonexistence of the Gradient of the Twisted Rabinowitz Action Functional

Let
(M,\lambda)
be an exact symplectic manifold and
\varphi\in\Symp(M,d\lambda)
a symplectomorphism of finite order. For
H\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that
H\circ\varphi=H
, one can define the twisted Rabinowitz action functional

\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}\colon\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},\hskip 22.99988pt\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}(\gamma,\tau)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{0}^{1}\gamma^{*}\lambda-\tau\int_{0}^{1}H(\gamma(t))dt.

(40)

Let
J
be a
d\lambda
-compatible almost complex structure such that
\varphi^{*}J=J
. Then one can consider the gradient of
\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}
with respect to the
L^{2}
-metric

\langle(X,\eta),(Y,\sigma)\rangle_{J}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda(JX(t),Y(t))dt+\eta\sigma

(41)

for all
(X,\eta),(Y,\sigma)\in T_{\gamma}\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\times\mathbb{R}
and
(\gamma,\tau)\in\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\times\mathbb{R}
.

Theorem B.1 (Nonexistence Gradient).

Let
(M,\lambda)
be a connected exact symplectic manifold,
\varphi\in\Symp(M,d\lambda)
of finite order and
H\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that
H\circ\varphi=H
. If
\varphi^{*}\lambda\neq\lambda
, then the gradient of the twisted Rabinowitz action functional (
40
) with respect to the
L^{2}
-metric (
41
) does not exist.

Proof.

Assume that the gradient
\grad\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\times\mathbb{R})
exists. We write

\grad\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}=\grad\mathscr{A}^{H}+V

for some
V\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M)
and

\grad\mathscr{A}^{H}|_{(\gamma,\tau)}=\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle J(\dot{\gamma}-\tau X_{H}(\gamma))\\
\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{1}H(\gamma(t))dt\end{pmatrix}

for all
(\gamma,\tau)\in\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\times\mathbb{R}
. Indeed, this follows from

d\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}|_{(\gamma,\tau)}(X,\eta)=d\mathscr{A}^{H}|_{(\gamma,\tau)}(X,\eta)+(\varphi^{*}\lambda-\lambda)(X(0))

(42)

for all
(X,\eta)\in T_{\gamma}\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M\times\mathbb{R}
, where

d\mathscr{A}^{H}|_{(\gamma,\tau)}(X,\eta)=\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda(X,\dot{\gamma}(t)-\tau X_{H}(\gamma(t)))dt-\eta\int_{0}^{1}H(\gamma(t))dt.

By assumption, there exists
x\in M
and
v\in T_{x}M
with
(\varphi^{*}\lambda)_{x}(v)\neq\lambda_{x}(v)
. As by assumption
M
is connected, there exists a smooth path
u\in C^{\infty}(\mathinner{[0,1]},M)
from
x
to
\varphi(x)
. Fix a smooth cutoff function
\beta\in C^{\infty}(\mathinner{[0,1]},\mathinner{[0,1]})
such that
\beta=0
in a neighbourhood of
0
and
\beta=1
in a neighbourhood of
1
. Then we can extend
u
by

\gamma(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\varphi^{k}(u(\beta(t-k)))\hskip 22.99988pt\forall t\in\mathinner{[k,k+1]},k\in\mathbb{Z}.

Clearly,
\gamma\in\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M
by construction. Extend
v\in T_{\gamma(0)}M
to
X_{v}\in T_{\gamma}\mathscr{L}_{\varphi}M
by

X_{v}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(1-\beta(t-k))P_{0,\beta(t-k)}^{\varphi^{k}\circ u}(D\varphi^{k}(v))+\beta(t-k)P_{1,\beta(t-k)}^{\varphi^{k}\circ u}(D\varphi^{k+1}(v)),

for all
t\in\mathinner{[k,k+1]}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
P
denotes the parallel transport system induced by the Levi–Civita connection associated with the metric
m_{J}
. Choose a sequence
(\beta_{j})\subseteq C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathinner{[0,1]})
with
\beta_{j}=1
on
\mathinner{\bigl[0,\frac{1}{2j}\bigr]}\cup\mathinner{\bigl[1-\frac{1}{2j},1\bigr]}
and such that
\supp\beta_{j}\subseteq\mathinner{\bigl[0,\frac{1}{j}\bigr]}\cup\mathinner{\bigl[1-\frac{1}{j},1\bigr]}
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Using (
42
) we compute

\displaystyle\langle V,\beta_{j}X_{v}\rangle_{J}

\displaystyle=\langle\grad\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi},\beta_{j}X_{v}\rangle_{J}-\langle\grad\mathscr{A}^{H},\beta_{j}X_{v}\rangle_{J}

\displaystyle=d\mathscr{A}^{H}_{\varphi}(\beta_{j}X_{v})-d\mathscr{A}^{H}(\beta_{j}X_{v})

\displaystyle=(\varphi^{*}\lambda-\lambda)(\beta_{j}(0)X_{v}(0))

\displaystyle=(\varphi^{*}\lambda-\lambda)(v)

for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
, implying

\displaystyle(\varphi^{*}\lambda-\lambda)(v)

\displaystyle=\lim_{j\to\infty}\langle V,\beta_{j}X_{v}\rangle_{J}

\displaystyle=\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda(JV(t),\beta_{j}(t)X_{v}(t))dt

\displaystyle=0

by dominated convergence.
∎

Appendix C
M-Polyfolds

The classical approach for establishing generic transversality results in Floer theories is via a suitable version of the Sard–Smale Theorem
[
MS12
, Theorem A.5.1]
. The idea is to represent the moduli space of negative gradient flow lines as the zero set of an appropriate Fredholm section. Unfortunately, this does not work for the moduli space of unparametrised negative gradient flow lines as the reparametrisation action is not smooth. Moreover, the transversality results usually require perturbing the given metric to a generic one. There is a more abstract approach for proving transversality results via polyfold theory. This theory was and is still developed by Hofer–Wysocki–Zehnder
[
HWZ21
]
primarily having symplectic field theory in mind. Another more algebraic approach to abstract perturbations is via Kuranishi structures developed by Fukaya–Oh–Ohta–Ono
[
Fuk+20
]
.

In the first section we introduce the basic terminology of polyfold theory, namely the notion of scale smoothness on scale Banach spaces.

In the second section we formulate a prototypical result for Morse–Bott homology following the brilliant lecture notes
[
Cie18
]
.

C.1
Scale Calculus

Definition C.1 (Scale Structure,
[
HWZ21
, Definition 1.1.1]
).

A
scale structure
on a Banach space
E
is a decreasing sequence

E=\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E_{0}\supseteq E_{1}\supseteq E_{2}\supseteq\dots

of Banach spaces such that the inclusion
E_{k+1}\hookrightarrow E_{k}
is compact for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and such that
E_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\cap_{k=0}^{\infty}E_{k}
is dense in every
E_{k}
. A Banach space
E
together with a scale structure
(E_{k})
is called a
scale Banach space
.

Example C.1 (Shifted Scale Banach Space,
[
FW21
, Definition 3.3]
).

Let
(E,(E_{k}))
be a scale Banach space and
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then
\mathinner{\bigl(E^{k},(E^{m}_{k})\bigr)}
is a scale Banach space where
E^{m}_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=E_{m+k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Example C.2 (Scale Direct Sum,
[
FW21
, Definition 3.4]
).

Let
(E,(E_{k}))
and
(F,(F_{k}))
be scale Banach spaces. Then
(E\oplus F,(E_{k}\oplus F_{k}))
is also a scale Banach space.

The following example underlies Morse and Floer theory.

Example C.3 (Weighted Sobolev Spaces,
[
FW21
, Example 3.9]
).

Fix a monotone cutoff function
\beta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},\mathinner{[-1,1]})
with

\beta(s)=\begin{cases}1&s\geq 1,\\
-1&s\leq-1,\end{cases}

and
\delta>0
. Define

\gamma_{\delta}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},\hskip 22.99988pt\gamma_{\delta}(s)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=e^{\delta\beta(s)s}.

For
p\in\mathinner{(1,+\infty)}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
define the
Sobolev spaces with weight
\delta
by

W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{u\in W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\gamma_{\delta}u\in W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n})\}.

Choose a strictly increasing sequence
(\delta_{k})
with
\delta_{0}=0
. Then
(E,(E_{k}))
is a scale Banach space with

E_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=W^{k,p}_{\delta_{k}}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n})\hskip 22.99988pt\forall k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Definition C.2 (Scale Continuity,
[
FW21
, Definition 4.1]
).

Let
(E,(E_{k}))
and
(F,(F_{k}))
be two scale Banach spaces. A map
f\colon E\to F
is called
scale continuous
, iff
f(E_{k})\subseteq F_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and the restriction
f|_{E_{k}}\colon E_{k}\to F_{k}
is continuous.

Definition C.3 (Tangent Bundle,
[
HWZ21
, Definition 1.1.14]
).

For a scale Banach space
(E,(E_{k}))
define its
tangent bundle
by
TE\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=E^{1}\oplus E
.

Definition C.4 (Scale Differentiability,
[
FW21
, Definition 4.2]
).

A scale continuous map
f\colon E\to F
between scale Banach spaces
(E,(E_{k}))
and
(F,(F_{k}))
is called
scale differentiable
, iff for every
x\in E_{1}
there exists a bounded linear operator

Df(x)\colon E_{0}\to F_{0},

called the
scale differential of
f
, such that the restriction
f|_{E_{1}}\colon E_{1}\to F_{0}
is Fréchet differentiable with derivative
Df|_{E_{1}}
and the
tangent map

Tf\colon TE\to TF,\hskip 22.99988ptTf(x,h)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(f(x),Df(x)h)

is scale continuous.

Using the iterated notion of scale differentiability one can define higher scale regularity. We say that a scale differentiable map is
scale smooth
, iff its tangent map is infinitely scale differentiable. The following example motivated the development of scale calculus.

Example C.4 (Weighted Sobolev Spaces,
[
FW21
, Theorem 6.2]
).

Let
E_{k}
be the scale of weighted Sobolev spaces introduced in Example
C.3
on the scale Banach space
E=L^{p}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then the shift map

\mathbb{R}\oplus E\to E,\hskip 22.99988pt(r,x)\mapsto x(\cdot+r)

is scale smooth.

One important property of scale differentiability is that the chain rule remains valid in this general setting.

Proposition C.1 (Chain Rule,
[
HWZ21
, Theorem 1.3.1]
).

Uppose that
f\colon E\to F
and
g\colon F\to G
are scale differentiable maps for scale Banach spaces
E
,
F
and
G
. Then the composition
g\circ f
is scale differentiable with tangent map

T(g\circ f)=Tg\circ Tf\colon TE\to TG.

The proof of the chain rule heavily relies on the compactness of the embeddings of the scales. Using the chain rule one is able to define the notion of scale manifolds and scale differential geometry in analogy to the finite-dimensional case. For details see
[
Cie18
, Chapter 5]
. Thus with the theory developed so far, one can make sense of the smooth moduli space of unparametrised negative gradient flow lines. However, for broken negative gradient flow lines one needs an even more general notion, including scale manifolds.

Definition C.5 (Retraction,
[
HWZ21
, Definition 2.1.1]
).

Let
E
be scale Banach space. A
retraction on
E
is a scale smooth map
r\colon E\to E
such that
r^{2}=r
.

Remark C.1.

If
X
is a smooth Banach manifold, then
\Fix(r)=r(M)
is a smooth submanifold for every smooth retraction
r\colon X\to X
by a result of Cartan
[
HWZ21
, Proposition 2.1.2]
.

It is in general not true that the fixed point set of a scale smooth retraction of a scale manifold is a scale submanifold. This lead Hofer–Wysocki–Zehnder to the generalised notion of an
M-polyfold
, where the “M” stands for “manifold flavoured”. Heuristically, an M-polyfold is locally the fixed point set of a scale smooth retraction of a scale smooth manifold. The main aspect for us is that Fredholm theory still is valid in M-polyfolds in some sense. For an extensive treatment see
[
HWZ21
, Part I]
.

C.2
M-Polyfold Setup for Morse–Bott Homology

In this section we explain how an M-polyfold Fredholm setup for Morse–Bott homology can be defined. We follow
[
Cie18
, Section 8.4]
. The essential arguments are contained in
[
Fra04
, Appendix A]
. We assume the following setup. Let
(M,g)
be a compact Riemannian manifold and
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
a Morse–Bott function. Choose an additional Morse function
h\in C^{\infty}(\Crit f)
and a Riemannian metric
g_{0}
on
\Crit f
such that
(h,g_{0})
is a Morse–Smale pair, that is, the stable and unstable manifolds intersect transversally. Pick two connected critical components
C^{\pm}\subseteq\Crit f
. For

0<\delta<\min\{\mathinner{\lvert\lambda\rvert}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\lambda\in\sigma(\Hess_{x}f))\setminus\{0\},x\in\Crit f\}

and a positive strictly increasing sequence
0<\delta_{0}<\delta_{1}<\dots<\delta
, consider the Banach manifold
\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}_{k}(C^{-},C^{+})
consisting of all maps
u\in H^{k+2}_{\delta_{k}}(\mathbb{R},M)
converging exponentially to
C^{\pm}
, that is, there exist
x^{\pm}\in C^{\pm}
as well as

\xi^{-}\in H^{k+2}_{\delta_{k}}(\mathinner{(-\infty,-T]},T_{x^{-}}M)\hskip 11.49994pt\text{and}\hskip 11.49994pt\xi^{+}\in H^{k+2}_{\delta_{k}}(\mathinner{[T,+\infty)},T_{x^{+}}M)

for some
T\in\mathbb{R}
with

u(s)=\exp_{x^{\pm}}(\xi^{\pm}(s))\hskip 22.99988pt\forall\pm s\geq T.

See Figure
12
. Local charts on
\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}_{k}(C^{-},C^{+})
are constructed by exponential neighbourhoods around smooth paths
[
Sch93
, Appendix A]
. Similar to
[
AW13
]
, one can construct scale smooth charts using those exponential neighbourhoods, equipping the Banach manifold
\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}(C_{-},C_{+})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}_{0}(C_{-},C_{+})
with a scale smooth structure. Moreover, there are two natural scale smooth evaluation maps

\ev^{\pm}\colon\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}(C^{-},C^{+})\to\Crit f,\hskip 22.99988pt\ev^{\pm}(u)=x^{\pm}.

Figure 12
:
An asymptotically exponential Sobolev path connecting two critical components of a Morse–Bott function.

Fix
x^{\pm}\in C^{\pm}\cap\Crit h
and define the stable and unstable manifolds by

W^{\pm}(x^{\pm})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\biggl\{x\in\Crit f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\lim_{s\to\pm\infty}\phi_{s}(x)=x^{\pm}\biggr\}},

where
\phi\colon\mathbb{R}\times\Crit f\to\Crit f
denotes the negative gradient flow of
h
with respect to the Morse–Smale metric
m_{0}
. Define

\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}_{k}^{1}(x^{-},x^{+})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\bigl\{u\in\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}_{k}(C^{-},C^{+})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\ev^{\pm}(u)\in W^{\pm}(x^{\pm})\bigr\}}

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. See Figure
13
. Again,
\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}^{1}(x_{-},x_{+})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}^{1}_{0}(x_{-},x_{+})
is a scale manifold. For details, see the proof of
[
Fra04
, Theorem A.12]
and
[
Fra04
, Theorem A.14]
.

Figure 13
:
A weighted Sobolev path with one cascade.

There exists a canonical scale smooth bundle

\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{1}(x^{-},x^{+})\to\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}^{1}(x^{-},x^{+}),

where the fibre over
u\in\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}_{k}^{1}(C^{-},C^{+})
is given by

\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{k}^{1}(x^{-},x^{+})|_{u}=H^{k+1}_{\delta_{k}}(\mathbb{R},u^{*}TM)

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

\widetilde{\partial}\colon\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}^{1}(x^{-},x^{+})\to\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{1}(x^{-},x^{+}),\hskip 22.99988pt\widetilde{\partial}(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\dot{u}+\grad_{m}f(u)

is a scale Fredholm operator. If
x^{-}\neq x^{+}
, the scale smooth reparametrisation action

\mathbb{R}\oplus\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}^{1}(x^{-},x^{+})\to\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}^{1}(x^{-},x^{+}),\hskip 22.99988pt(r,u)\mapsto u(\cdot+r),

as in Example
C.4
is free, giving rise to a scale smooth bundle

\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{1}(x^{-},x^{+})/\mathbb{R}\to\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}^{1}(x^{-},x^{+})/\mathbb{R}

to which
\widetilde{\partial}
descends to a scale Fredholm section
\partial
. This construction can be generalised to
m
cascades and thus yields the scale smooth bundle

\mathscr{F}^{m}(x^{-},x^{+})\to\mathscr{E}^{m}(x^{-},x^{+})

of unparametrised paths from
x^{-}
to
x^{+}
with
m
cascades. Now we consider broken paths. For
x^{0},\dots,x^{l}\in\Crit h
with

f(x^{0})\geq f(x^{1})\geq\dots\geq f(x^{l-1})\geq f(x^{l}),

we define the space of broken paths from
x^{0}
to
x^{l}
by

\mathscr{E}(x^{0},\dots,x^{l})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\coprod_{(\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{l})\in\mathbb{N}^{l+1}_{0}}\mathscr{E}^{\alpha_{0}}(x^{0},x^{1})\times\dots\times\mathscr{E}^{\alpha_{l}}(x^{l-1},x^{l}).

Then
\mathscr{E}(x^{0},\dots,x^{l})
is a scale manifold over which we have the scale bundle

\mathscr{F}(x^{0},\dots,x^{l})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\coprod_{(\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{l})\in\mathbb{N}^{l+1}_{0}}\mathscr{F}^{\alpha_{0}}(x^{0},x^{1})\times\dots\times\mathscr{F}^{\alpha_{l}}(x^{l-1},x^{l})

and the scale Fredholm section

\partial\times\dots\times\partial\colon\mathscr{E}(x^{0},\dots,x^{l})\to\mathscr{F}(x^{0},\dots,x^{l}).

For
x^{\pm}\in\Crit h
with
f(x^{-})\geq f(x^{+})
we define the space of broken paths from
x^{-}
to
x^{+}
by

X(x^{-},x^{+})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\coprod_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{1},\dots,x^{l-1}\in\Crit h\\
f(x^{-})\geq f(x^{1})\geq\dots\geq f(x^{l-1})\geq f(x^{+})\end{subarray}}\mathscr{E}(x^{-},x^{1},\dots,x^{l-1},x^{+}).

Similarly, one defines

Y(x^{-},x^{+})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\coprod_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{1},\dots,x^{l-1}\in\Crit h\\
f(x^{-})\geq f(x^{1})\geq\dots\geq f(x^{l-1})\geq f(x^{+})\end{subarray}}\mathscr{F}(x^{-},x^{1},\dots,x^{l-1},x^{+}).

One can show that
X(x^{-},x^{+})
carries the natural structure of an M-polyfold and

Y(x^{-},x^{+})\to X(x^{-},x^{+})

is a bundle. Moreover, the scale Fredholm section above induces a scale Fredholm section

\partial\colon X(x^{-},x^{+})\to Y(x^{-},x^{+})

of index

\ind\partial=\ind(x^{-})-\ind(x^{+})-1,

where either

\ind=\ind_{f}+\ind_{h}\hskip 22.99988pt\text{or}\hskip 22.99988pt\ind=-\frac{1}{2}\sgn\Hess f-\frac{1}{2}\sgn\Hess h.

For details see
[
CF09
, Appendix A]
. One can show that the level set
\partial^{-1}(0)
of unparametrised broken negative gradient flow lines is compact and that there exists an abstract perturbation of
\partial
, that is, a scale smooth section
\sigma\colon X\to Y
, where

X\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{\pm}\in\Crit h\\
f(x^{-})\geq f(x^{+})\end{subarray}}X(x^{-},x^{+})\hskip 22.99988pt\text{and}\hskip 22.99988ptY\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{\pm}\in\Crit h\\
f(x^{-})\geq f(x^{+})\end{subarray}}Y(x^{-},x^{+}),

with the property that for all
x^{\pm}\in\Crit h
the moduli space

\mathscr{M}(x^{-},x^{+})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{u\in X(x^{-},x^{+})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(\partial+\sigma)(u)=0\}

is compact and
\partial+\sigma
is transverse to the zero section. Hence we arrive at a prototypical result for Morse–Bott homology, compare
[
Cie18
, Corollary 8.9]
.

Theorem C.1 (M-Polyfold Setup for Morse–Bott Homology).

Let
(M,g)
be a compact Riemannian manifold without boundary and
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
a Morse–Bott function. Choose an additional Morse function
h\in C^{\infty}(\Crit f)
and an additional Riemannian metric
g_{0}
on
\Crit f
such that
(h,g_{0})
is a Morse–Smale pair. For all critical points
x^{\pm}\in\Crit h
, the moduli space

\mathscr{M}(x^{-},x^{+})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{u\in X(x^{-},x^{+})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(\partial+\sigma)(u)=0\}

is a smooth compact manifold with corners of dimension

\dim\mathscr{M}(x^{-},x^{+})=\ind(x^{-})-\ind(x^{+})-1.

Moreover, there is a canoncial diffeomorphism

\partial\mathscr{M}(x^{-},x^{+})=\mathscr{M}(x^{-},x^{+})\cap\partial X\cong\coprod_{x\in\Crit h}\mathscr{M}(x^{-},x)\times\mathscr{M}(x,x^{+}).

Appendix D
Bubbling Analysis

In this section we prove the main result about the compactness of the moduli space of negative gradient flow lines of the symplectic action functional.

Definition D.1 (Symplectic Asphericity).

A connected symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
is said to be
symplectically aspherical
, if

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}f^{*}\omega=0\hskip 22.99988pt\forall f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2},M).

Remark D.1.

A symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
is symplectically aspherical, if and only if
[\omega]|_{\pi_{2}(M)}=0
, where
[\omega]\in\operatorname{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{2}(M;\mathbb{R})
denotes the cohomology class of the closed form
\omega
.

Theorem D.1 (Bubbling).

Let
(M,\omega)
be a compact symplectically aspherical symplectic manifold and let
(u_{k})
be a sequence of negative gradient flow lines of the symplectic action functional
\mathcal{A}_{H}
for some
H\in C^{\infty}(M\times\mathbb{T})
with uniformly bounded energy

E_{J}(u_{k})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{s}u_{k}\rVert}^{2}_{J}

for some, and hence every,
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
J
. Then the derivatives of
(u_{k})
are uniformly bounded.

The main idea of the proof is to assume that the derivatives
(Du_{k})
explode and then to construct a nonconstant
J
-holomorphic sphere. Indeed, assume that there exists a sequence
(s_{k},t_{k})
in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
such that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{s}u_{k}(s_{k},t_{k})\rVert}\to+\infty.

Then we rescale the sequence
(u_{k})
, see Figure
14
. Set

m_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{s}u_{k}(s_{k},t_{k})\rVert}\hskip 22.99988pt\text{and}\hskip 22.99988ptv_{k}(\sigma,\tau)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=u_{k}\mathinner{\biggl(\frac{\sigma}{m_{k}}+s_{k},\frac{\tau}{m_{k}}+t_{k}\biggr)}

for all
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathbb{C}
.

Then we compute

\displaystyle m_{k}\partial_{\sigma}v_{k}(\sigma,\tau)

\displaystyle=\partial_{s}u_{k}\mathinner{\biggl(\frac{\sigma}{m_{k}}+s_{k},\frac{\tau}{m_{k}}+t_{k}\biggr)},

\displaystyle m_{k}\partial_{\tau}v_{k}(\sigma,\tau)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}u_{k}\mathinner{\biggl(\frac{\sigma}{m_{k}}+s_{k},\frac{\tau}{m_{k}}+t_{k}\biggr)}.

In particular
\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{\sigma}v_{k}(0,0)\rVert}=1
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover, every
v_{k}
solves

\partial_{\sigma}v_{k}(\sigma,\tau)+J\partial_{\tau}v_{k}(\sigma,\tau)=\frac{1}{m_{k}}JX_{H_{\frac{\tau}{m_{k}}+t_{k}}}(v_{k}(\sigma,\tau))\hskip 22.99988pt\forall(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathbb{C}

as
u_{k}
satisfies the Floer equation (
5
). If there exists
v_{\infty}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C},M)
such that

v_{k}\xrightarrow{C^{\infty}_{\loc}}v_{\infty},\hskip 22.99988ptk\to\infty,

modulo subsequences, then
v_{\infty}
satisfies

\partial_{\sigma}v_{\infty}(\sigma,\tau)+J\partial_{\tau}v_{\infty}(\sigma,\tau)=0\hskip 22.99988pt\forall(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathbb{C}.

Consequently,
v_{\infty}
is a
J
-holomorphic plane. Using the assumption that the energy of the sequence
(u_{k})
is uniformly bounded, one can extend
v_{\infty}
to a
J
-holomorphic sphere
v\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2},M)
such that
v|_{\mathbb{C}}=v_{\infty}
via the identification
\mathbb{S}^{2}\cong\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Figure 14
:
Looking at the sequence
(u_{k})
of negative gradient flow lines of the symplectic action functional with a magnifying glass via rescaling.

D.1
Rescaling

Lemma D.1 (Hofer,
[
AH19
, Lemma 4.4.4]
).

Let
(X,d)
be a complete metric space and

f\colon X\to\mathinner{[0,+\infty)}

continuous. Given
\varepsilon>0
and
x\in X
, there exist
0<\delta\leq\varepsilon
and
y\in X
such that

d(x,y)\leq 2\delta,\hskip 22.99988pt\delta f(y)\geq\varepsilon f(x),\hskip 22.99988pt\text{and}\hskip 22.99988pt\sup_{z\in\overline{B}_{\delta}(y)}f(z)\leq 2f(y).

Lemma D.2 (Rescaling).

Let
(u_{k})
be a sequence of negative gradient flow lines of the symplectic action functional
\mathcal{A}_{H}
for some
H\in C^{\infty}(M\times\mathbb{T})
and
J
some
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure. Then there exists a sequence
(R_{k})
in
\mathinner{(0,+\infty)}
with
R_{k}\to+\infty
and a sequence
v_{k}\in C^{\infty}(B_{R_{k}}(0),M)
such that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{\sigma}v_{k}+J\partial_{\tau}v_{k}\rVert}_{\infty}=0\hskip 22.99988pt\text{and}\hskip 22.99988pt1\leq\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{\sigma}v_{k}\rVert}_{\infty}\leq 2.

Proof.

Abbreviate
z_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(s_{k},t_{k})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
. Apply Hofer’s Lemma
D.1
with

f\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\to\mathinner{[0,+\infty)},\hskip 22.99988ptf(s,t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{s}u_{k}(s,t)\rVert},

as well as

\varepsilon_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m_{k}}},\hskip 22.99988pt\text{and}\hskip 22.99988ptx_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=z_{k}.

Thus there exists a nearby sequence
z^{\prime}_{k}=(s^{\prime}_{k},t^{\prime}_{k})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
and
0<\delta_{k}\leq\varepsilon_{k}
with

\delta_{k}\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{s}u_{k}(z^{\prime}_{k})\rVert}\geq\varepsilon_{k}\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{s}u_{k}(z_{k})\rVert}=\sqrt{m}_{k}

and

\sup_{z\in B_{\delta_{k}}(z_{k}^{\prime})}\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{s}u_{k}(z)\rVert}\leq 2\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{s}u_{k}(z^{\prime}_{k})\rVert}.

Rescale
m_{k}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{s}u_{k}(z_{k}^{\prime})\rVert}
, set
R_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\delta_{k}m_{k}^{\prime}
and

v_{k}(\sigma,\tau)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=u_{k}\mathinner{\biggl(\frac{\sigma}{m_{k}^{\prime}}+s_{k}^{\prime},\frac{\tau}{m_{k}^{\prime}}+t_{k}^{\prime}\biggr)}\hskip 22.99988pt\forall(\sigma,\tau)\in B_{R_{k}}(0).

As
u_{k}
is a solution to the Floer equation (
5
),
v_{k}
satisfies the equation

\partial_{\sigma}v_{k}(\sigma,\tau)+J\partial_{\tau}v_{k}(\sigma,\tau)=\frac{1}{m_{k}^{\prime}}JX_{H_{\frac{\tau}{m_{k}^{\prime}}+t_{k}^{\prime}}}(v_{k}(\sigma,\tau))

(43)

for all
(\sigma,\tau)\in B_{R_{k}}(0)
. As
m^{\prime}_{k}\geq m_{k}\to+\infty
, we conclude

\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{\sigma}v_{k}+J\partial_{\tau}v_{k}\rVert}_{\infty}=0.

Finally, we compute

\displaystyle\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{\sigma}v_{k}\rVert}_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\sup_{z\in B_{R_{k}}(0)}\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{\sigma}v_{k}(z)\rVert}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m_{k}^{\prime}}\sup_{z\in B_{\delta_{k}}(z_{k}^{\prime})}\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{s}u_{k}(z)\rVert}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{m_{k}^{\prime}}\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{s}u_{k}(z^{\prime}_{k})\rVert}

\displaystyle=2,

and

m_{k}^{\prime}\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{\sigma}v_{k}(0,0)\rVert}=\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{s}u_{k}(z_{k}^{\prime})\rVert}=m_{k}^{\prime}.

This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma D.3.

Let
(v_{k})
be the sequence constructed in the Rescaling Lemma
D.2
. Then there exists
v_{\infty}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{C},M)
such that

v_{k}\xrightarrow{C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}}v_{\infty},\hskip 22.99988ptk\to\infty,

up to a subsequence.

Proof.

By the Rescaling Lemma
D.2
and compactness of
M
there exists
C>0
such that

\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{\tau}v_{k}\rVert}_{\infty}\leq C\hskip 22.99988pt\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.

Fix
R>0
and choose
K_{R}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
R_{k}\geq R
for all
k\geq K_{R}
and consider the restrictions
v_{k}|_{B_{R}(0)}
for all
k\geq K_{R}
. As the derivatives of
v_{k}
are uniformly bounded, we conclude that
v_{k}|_{B_{R}(0)}
is of Sobolev class

W^{1,\infty}(B_{R}(0))\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{f\in W^{1,\infty}(B_{R}(0),\mathbb{R}^{4n+1})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}f(B_{R}(0))\subseteq M\}

for all
k\geq K_{k}
where we consider
M^{2n}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{4n+1}
via the Whitney embedding Theorem. Thus by Morrey’s inequality
[
Bre11
, Corollary 9.14]
, every
v_{k}|_{B_{R}(0)}
is of Hölder class
C^{0,1}(B_{R}(0))
, and hence
v_{k}|_{B_{R}(0)}
is equicontinuous. As
M
is compact, Ascolis Theorem implies the existence of
v^{R}\in C^{0}(B_{R}(0),M)
with

v_{k}|_{B_{R}(0)}\xrightarrow{C^{0}}v^{R},\hskip 22.99988ptk\to\infty,

up to a subsequence. Choose a subsequence
(k_{j}^{1})
with
k_{j}^{1}\geq K_{1}
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and such that there exists
v^{1}\in C^{0}(B_{1}(0),M)
with

v_{k_{j}^{1}}|_{B_{1}(0)}\xrightarrow{C^{0}}v^{1},\hskip 22.99988ptj\to\infty.

Inductively, choose a subsequence
(k_{j}^{\mu+1})
of
(k_{j}^{\mu})
for all
\mu\in\mathbb{N}
with
k_{j}^{\mu+1}\geq K_{\mu+1}
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and such that there exists
v^{\mu+1}\in C^{0}(B_{\mu+1}(0),M)
with

v_{k^{\mu+1}_{j}}|_{B_{\mu+1}(0)}\xrightarrow{C^{0}}v^{\mu+1},\hskip 22.99988ptj\to\infty.

Finally, taking the diagonal subsequence yields

v_{k^{j}_{j}}\xrightarrow{C^{0}_{\loc}}v_{\infty}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{C},M),\hskip 22.99988ptj\to\infty.

∎

D.2
Elliptic Bootstrapping

Lemma D.4 (Elliptic Bootstrapping).

Denote by
v_{\infty}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{C},M)
the map constructed in Lemma
D.3
. Then
v_{\infty}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C},M)
is a nonconstant
J
-holomorphic plane.

Proof.

Fix sequences
(z_{j})\subseteq\mathbb{C}
and
r_{j}\subseteq\mathinner{(0,+\infty)}
such that

•

v_{\infty}|_{B_{4r_{j}}(z_{j})}
is contained in a chart
U_{j}
of
M
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}B_{r_{j}}(z_{j})=\mathbb{C}
.

•

for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
K_{j}
such that
v_{k}|_{B_{2r_{j}}(z_{j})}\subseteq U_{j}
for all
k\geq K_{j}
.

Fix smooth bump functions
\beta_{j}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C},\mathinner{[0,1]})
for
\overline{B}_{r_{j}}(z_{j})
supported in
B_{2r_{j}}(z_{j})
and define

\overline{v}_{k}^{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\beta_{j}v_{k}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}^{2n})

for all
k,j\in\mathbb{N}
. We compute

\Delta\overline{v}^{j}_{k}=(\Delta\beta_{j})v_{k}+2\partial_{\sigma}\beta_{j}\partial_{\sigma}v_{k}+2\partial_{\tau}\beta_{j}\partial_{\tau}v_{k}+\beta_{j}\Delta v_{k}.

(44)

To compute
\Delta v_{k}
, we differentiate (
43
) in charts. Applying
\partial_{\sigma}
we get that

\partial_{\sigma}^{2}v_{k}(\sigma,\tau)+DJ\partial_{\sigma}v_{k}(\sigma,\tau)\partial_{\tau}v_{k}(\sigma,\tau)+J\partial_{\sigma}\partial_{\tau}v_{k}(\sigma,\tau)

is equal to

\frac{1}{m_{k}^{\prime}}D\grad_{J}H_{\frac{\tau}{m_{k}^{\prime}}+t_{k}^{\prime}}(v_{k}(\sigma,\tau))\partial_{\sigma}v_{k}(\sigma,\tau),

where
\grad_{J}H_{t}
denotes the gradient of
H_{t}
with respect to the Riemannian metric induced by
J
, that is, the Riemannian metric
\omega(J\cdot,\cdot)
. Applying
\partial_{\tau}
to (
43
) we get that

\partial_{\tau}\partial_{\sigma}v_{k}(\sigma,\tau)+J\partial_{\tau}^{2}v_{k}(\sigma,\tau)+DJ(\partial_{\tau}v_{k}(\sigma,\tau))^{2}

is equal to

\frac{1}{m_{k}^{\prime}}D\grad_{J}H_{\frac{\tau}{m_{k}^{\prime}}+t_{k}^{\prime}}(v_{k}(\sigma,\tau))\partial_{\tau}v_{k}(\sigma,\tau)+\frac{1}{m_{k}^{\prime}}\partial_{\tau}\grad_{J}H_{\frac{\tau}{m_{k}^{\prime}}+t_{k}^{\prime}}(v_{k}(\sigma,\tau)).

Hence

\Delta v_{k}=P(\partial_{\sigma}v_{k},\partial_{\tau}v_{k}),

where
P
is a polynomial of degree
2
with
C^{\infty}
-coefficients and so

\Delta\overline{v}_{k}^{j}=P(\partial_{\sigma}\overline{v}_{k}^{j},\partial\overline{v}_{k}^{j})

by (
44
). As the derivatives
\partial_{\sigma}v_{k}
and
\partial_{\tau}v_{k}
are uniformly bounded, we conclude that
\Delta\overline{v}_{k}^{j}
is also uniformly bounded. Thus
\mathinner{\lVert\Delta\overline{v}_{k}^{j}\rVert}_{L^{p}}
is uniformly bounded for all exponents
p\in\mathinner{[1,+\infty]}
as
\Delta\overline{v}_{k}^{j}
is compactly supported by construction. Therefore, the Calderon–Zygmund inequality
[
MS12
, Corollary B.2.7]
implies that
\mathinner{\lVert\overline{v}_{k}^{j}\rVert}_{W^{2,p}}
is uniformly bounded for all
1<p<+\infty
. In particular,

\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{\sigma}\overline{v}_{k}^{j}\rVert}_{W^{1,p}}\hskip 22.99988pt\text{and}\hskip 22.99988pt\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{\tau}\overline{v}_{k}^{j}\rVert}_{W^{1,p}}

are uniformly bounded. Thus again by Morrey’s inequality,
\partial_{\sigma}\overline{v}_{k}^{j}
and
\partial_{\tau}\overline{v}_{k}^{j}
belong to the Hölder class
C^{0,\alpha}
for all
0<\alpha<1
. By Ascolis Theorem,
\partial_{\sigma}\overline{v}_{k}^{j}
and
\partial_{\tau}\overline{v}_{k}^{j}
admit convergent subsequences and

\overline{v}_{\infty}^{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\beta_{j}v_{\infty}\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}^{2n})

satisfies

v_{k}|_{B_{r_{j}}(z_{j})}\xrightarrow{C^{1}}\overline{v}_{\infty}^{j}

for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
.
Hence we have showed that
v_{\infty}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{C},M)
and

v_{k}\xrightarrow{C^{1}_{\loc}}v_{\infty}

up to subsequences. This procedure can be generalised to higher derivatives and is referred to as
elliptic bootstrapping
. Note that taking higher order derivatives makes only sense locally in a chart if we do not refer to a particular connection. Thus we get
v_{\infty}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C},M)
and

v_{k}\xrightarrow{C^{\infty}_{\loc}}v_{\infty},\hskip 22.99988ptk\to\infty,

up to subsequences. Finally,
v_{\infty}
is nonconstant as

\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{\sigma}v_{\infty}\rVert}_{\infty}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{\sigma}v_{k}\rVert}_{\infty}\geq 1

and satisfies

\partial_{\sigma}v_{\infty}+J\partial_{\tau}v_{\infty}=0

by the Rescaling Lemma
D.2
.
∎

D.3
Removal of Singularities

Consider the conformal diffeomorphism

\varphi\colon\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T},\hskip 22.99988pt\varphi(re^{2\pi i\theta})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Log(re^{2\pi i\theta}),

and define

w\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\to M,\hskip 22.99988ptw\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=v_{\infty}\circ\varphi^{-1},

where
v_{\infty}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C},M)
denotes the nonconstant
J
-holomorphic plane constructed in Lemma
D.3
. Then

\lim_{s\to-\infty}w(s,\cdot)=v_{\infty}(0)

and
w
is of finite energy. Indeed, we compute

E_{J}(w)=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}}w^{*}\omega=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{1}\omega(J\partial_{s}w(s,t),\partial_{s}w(s,t))dtds\leq\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}E_{J}(u_{k})

by
[
MS12
, Lemma 2.2.1]
. Moreover,
w
is a negative gradient flow line of the symplectic area functional

\mathscr{A}\colon\Lambda M\to\mathbb{R},\hskip 22.99988pt\mathscr{A}(\gamma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathbb{D}}\overline{\gamma}^{*}\omega.

Lemma D.5.

For every sequence
(r_{k})\subseteq\mathinner{(0,+\infty)}
with
r_{k}\to+\infty
as
k\to\infty
define a sequence
(w_{k})
by

w_{k}(s,t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=w(s+r_{k},t)\hskip 22.99988pt\forall(s,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}.

Then there exists a point
w_{\infty}\in M
such that

w_{k}\xrightarrow{C^{\infty}_{\loc}}w_{\infty},\hskip 22.99988ptk\to\infty,

up to a subsequence.

Proof.

By
[
MS12
, p. 89–90]
there exists a constant
a\geq 0
such that

\Delta e\geq-ae^{2},

where

e\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\to\mathinner{[0,+\infty)},\hskip 22.99988pte(s,t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{s}w(s,t)\rVert}^{2}

denotes the energy density. As
E_{J}(w)<+\infty
, there exists
R>0
such that

\int_{R}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{1}e(s,t)dtds\leq\min\mathinner{\biggl\{\frac{\pi}{8a},1\biggr\}}

and

\int_{-\infty}^{-R}\int_{0}^{1}e(s,t)dtds\leq\min\mathinner{\biggl\{\frac{\pi}{8a},1\biggr\}}.

Let
z=(s,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
such that
\mathinner{\lvert s\rvert}\geq R+1
. Then

\int_{B_{1}(z)}e<\frac{\pi}{8a}.

By
[
MS12
, Lemma 4.3.3]
we conclude

e(z)\leq\frac{8}{\pi}\int_{B_{1}(z)}e\leq\frac{8}{\pi}.

Consequently,
e
is uniformly bounded, as
e|_{\mathinner{[-R-1,R+1]}\times\mathbb{T}}
is uniformly bounded by continuity of
e
. Hence
\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{s}w\rVert}
is uniformly bounded by definition of
e
and
\mathinner{\lVert\partial_{t}w\rVert}
is uniformly bounded as
w
is a
J
-holomorphic curve. By an eliptic bootrstrapping argument as in Lemma
D.4
, we conclude

w_{k}\xrightarrow{C^{\infty}_{\loc}}w_{\infty},\hskip 22.99988ptk\to\infty,

up to a subsequence, where
w_{\infty}
is a negative gradient flow line of the symplectic area functional
\mathscr{A}
. Then
w_{\infty}
is constant. Indeed, assume that
w_{\infty}
is not constant. Then there exists
s<s^{\prime}
such that

\varepsilon\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathscr{A}(w_{\infty}(s))-\mathscr{A}(w_{\infty}(s^{\prime}))>0.

Moreover, there exists
K\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathscr{A}(w_{k}(s))-\mathscr{A}(w_{k}(s^{\prime}))=\mathscr{A}(w(s+r_{k}))-\mathscr{A}(w(s^{\prime}+r_{k}))\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}

for all
k\geq K
. Define a subsequence
(r_{k_{j}})
of
(r_{k})
recursively by

k_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=K\hskip 22.99988pt\text{and}\hskip 22.99988ptk_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\min\{l\in\mathbb{N}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}s^{\prime}+r_{k_{j-1}}\leq s+r_{l}\}.

This works as
r_{k}\to+\infty
as
k\to\infty
. Fix
l\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we compute

\displaystyle E(w)

\displaystyle=\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\mathscr{A}(w(s))-\inf_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\mathscr{A}(w(s))

\displaystyle\geq\mathscr{A}(w(s+r_{k_{0}}))-\mathscr{A}(w(s+r_{k_{l}}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu=1}^{l}\mathinner{\bigl(\mathscr{A}(w(s+r_{k_{\nu-1}}))-\mathscr{A}(w(s+r_{k_{\nu}}))\bigr)}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{\nu=1}^{l}\mathinner{\bigl(\mathscr{A}(w(s+r_{k_{\nu-1}}))-\mathscr{A}(w(s^{\prime}+r_{k_{\nu-1}}))\bigr)}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\varepsilon l}{2}.

As
l\in\mathbb{N}
was arbitrary, we conlcude that
E(w)=+\infty
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
D.1
.

As
M
is compact, there exists a finite open cover
U_{1},\dots,U_{m}
of
M
such that
\overline{U}_{j}
is contained in a Darboux chart. Define

\mathscr{U}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{\gamma\in\Lambda M\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\gamma(\mathbb{T})\subseteq U_{j}\text{ for some $j=1,\dots,m$}\}.

Step 1: There exists
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
w(s)\in\mathscr{U}
for all
s\geq s_{0}
.
Assume that there exists a sequence
(r_{k})\subseteq\mathinner{(0,+\infty)}
with
r_{k}\to+\infty
as
k\to\infty
and
w(r_{k})\notin\mathscr{U}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. By Lemma
D.5
, there exists
w_{\infty}\in M
such that

w_{k}\xrightarrow{C^{\infty}_{\loc}}w_{\infty},\hskip 22.99988ptk\to\infty,

up to a subsequence. But
w_{\infty}\in U_{j}
for some
j
and thus there exists
K\in\mathbb{N}
such that
w(r_{k},t)\in U_{j}
for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
and
k\geq K
. Consequently,
w(r_{k})\in\mathscr{U}
for all
k\geq K
.

Step 2: There exist constants
C>0
and
\kappa>0
such that

d_{L^{2}}(w(s),w_{\infty})\leq Ce^{-\kappa s}\hskip 22.99988pt\forall s\geq s_{0},

where
w_{\infty}
is the limit of
w(k)=w_{k}(0)
as
k\to\infty
up to a subsequence
(k_{j})
.
By
[
FN20
, Proposition 6.4]
and
[
FN20
, Lemma 6.3]
, we have the action-energy inequality

\mathinner{\lvert\mathscr{A}(\gamma)\rvert}\leq C_{0}\mathinner{\lVert\grad_{J}\mathscr{A}(\gamma)\rVert}_{J}^{2}\hskip 22.99988pt\forall\gamma\in\mathscr{U}

for some constant
C_{0}>0
as the symplectic area functional
\mathscr{A}
is Morse–Bott. See Remark
2.1
. Let
s\geq s_{0}
. Choose
j_{0}
such that
k_{j_{0}}>s
. We estimate

\mathscr{A}(w(s))>\mathscr{A}(w(k_{j_{0}}))\geq\lim_{j\to\infty}\mathscr{A}(w(k_{j}))=\lim_{j\to\infty}\mathscr{A}(w_{k_{j}}(0))=\mathscr{A}(w_{\infty})=0.

Let
s_{2}>s_{1}>s_{0}
. Using
[
FN20
, Lemma 6.5]
and
[
FN20
, Lemma 6.6]
we compute

\displaystyle d(w(s_{1}),w(s_{2}))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{C_{0}}}\mathinner{\bigl(\sqrt{\mathscr{A}(w(s_{1}))}-\sqrt{\mathscr{A}(w(s_{2}))}\bigr)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{C_{0}}}\sqrt{\mathscr{A}(w(s_{1}))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{C_{0}}}\sqrt{\mathscr{A}(w(s_{0}))}e^{\frac{1}{2C_{0}}(s_{0}-s_{1})}

Choose
j_{1}
such that
k_{j_{1}}\geq s_{1}
. Then for all
j\geq j_{1}
, we have that

d_{L^{2}}(w(s_{1}),w(k_{j}))\leq Ce^{-\kappa s_{1}},

where

C\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{C_{0}}}\sqrt{\mathscr{A}(w(s_{0}))}e^{\frac{1}{2C_{0}}s_{0}}\hskip 22.99988pt\text{and}\hskip 22.99988pt\kappa\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{2C_{0}}.

Thus

d_{L^{2}}(w(s_{1}),w_{\infty})=\lim_{j\to\infty}d_{L^{2}}(w(s_{1}),w(k_{j}))\leq Ce^{-\kappa s_{1}}.

Step 3: There exists a unique point
w_{\infty}\in M
such that

w(s)\xrightarrow{C^{\infty}}w_{\infty},\hskip 22.99988pts\to+\infty.

Uniqueness follows immediately from the exponential decay established in Step 2. Indeed, a priori,
w_{\infty}
does depend on the choice of subsequence
(k_{j})
. Let
w_{\infty}^{\prime}\in M
be the limit of a different subsequence. Then we compute

d_{L^{2}}(w_{\infty},w_{\infty}^{\prime})\leq d_{L^{2}}(w(s),w_{\infty})+d_{L^{2}}(w(s),w_{\infty}^{\prime})\leq Ce^{-\kappa s}+C^{\prime}e^{-\kappa^{\prime}s}\to 0

as
s\to+\infty
. That the limit can also be taken with respect to the
C^{\infty}
-topology follows from
[
AD14
, Proposition 6.5.3]
.
∎

Corollary D.1.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectically aspherical symplectic manifold and let
(u_{k},\tau_{k})
be a sequence of solutions
(u_{k},\tau_{k})\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T},M)\times C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
of

\begin{cases}\partial_{s}u_{k}(s,t)+J(\partial_{t}u_{k}(s,t)-\tau_{k}(s)X_{H}(u_{k}(s,t)))=0,\\
\displaystyle\partial_{s}\tau_{k}(s)=\int_{0}^{1}H(u_{k}(s,t))dt,\end{cases}

for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
for some
H\in C^{\infty}(M)
, with uniformly bounded energy. If there exists a compact subset
K\subseteq M\times\mathbb{R}
such that

\im(u_{k},\tau_{k})\subseteq K\hskip 22.99988pt\forall k\in\mathbb{N},

then the derivatives of
(u_{k})
are uniformly bounded.

Proof.

Crucial is the observation that under the above assumptions, the limit of (
43
) is still a
J
-holomorphic curve.
∎
